final answer
The final rate is computed per item in `calculate_item_rate`, a nested function of the `calculate_taxes_and_totals` class in `erpnext/controllers/taxes_and_totals.py`. Its caller is `calculate_item_values`. I read only the search excerpts, not the surrounding class. So I haven't seen `calculate_item_values` itself, and I haven't confirmed that `remove_margin` and `remove_discount` do what their names say. **Server side: `calculate_item_rate(item)`** 1. **No price list rate.** If `item.price_list_rate` is empty, it calls `remove_margin` and `remove_discount`, sets `rate_with_margin = 0` and returns. The rate is left alone. 2. **Applied pricing rules.** `has_pricing_rules` is true when `item.pricing_rules` is set and `doc.ignore_pricing_rule` is not. - When true, it calls `remove_margin(item)`. - It then loops over `get_applied_pricing_rules(item.pricing_rules)` (from `accounts/doctype/pricing_rule/utils.py`) and loads each Pricing Rule with `frappe.get_cached_doc`. - A rule's `margin_type` and `margin_rate_or_amount` are copied onto the item. This happens only if the rule has both fields and is either a Percentage margin or in the document's currency. - The rule's price and discount effects are applied earlier, in `pricing_rule.py::apply_price_discount_rule`. That function can overwrite `price_list_rate` with the rule's rate, set `discount_percentage` or `discount_amount`, and set the margin fields. 3. **Margin.** `item.rate_with_margin = get_rate_with_margin(item)`. - With no `margin_type`, the result is `price_list_rate`. - With `Percentage`, it is `price_list_rate * (1 + margin_rate_or_amount / 100)`. - Otherwise it is `price_list_rate + margin_rate_or_amount`. - The result is rounded to the `rate_with_margin` precision. 4. **Discount.** If `discount_percentage > 0`, then `discount_amount = rate_with_margin * discount_percentage / 100`. The percentage always overrides the amount. 5. **Calculated rate.** `calculated_rate = flt(rate_with_margin - discount_amount, precision("rate"))`. **Manual rate override** After step 5, the function compares `item.rate` with `calculated_rate`: - **Rate is 0 or pricing rules apply.** `item.rate = calculated_rate` and it returns. Pricing rules therefore beat a manual rate. - **Rate equals `calculated_rate`.** It returns, since margin and discount are already consistent. - **Rate differs and is above `price_list_rate`.** The manual rate is kept, and the difference is recorded as a margin. It sets `margin_type = "Amount"` and `margin_rate_or_amount = rate - price_list_rate`. It sets `rate_with_margin = rate` and calls `remove_discount`. - **Rate differs and is at or below `price_list_rate`.** The manual rate is kept, and the difference is recorded as a discount. It sets `rate_with_margin = price_list_rate` and `discount_amount = rate_with_margin - rate`. It sets `discount_percentage = 0` and calls `remove_margin`. **Client side** - **`taxes_and_totals.js::apply_pricing_rule_on_item(item)`** runs the same chain in the browser and writes the result with `frappe.model.set_value(..., "rate", ...)`. - It starts from `price_list_rate`, or `blanket_order_rate` for Sales Order and Quotation. - It adds the margin to get `rate_with_margin`, then derives `discount_amount` from `discount_percentage`. - The rate is `rate_with_margin - discount_amount`. - **`transaction.js`** calls it from the `price_list_rate`, `margin_type` and `margin_rate_or_amount` handlers. `price_list_rate` then calls `calculate_taxes_and_totals`. For other child doctypes it uses a simple `price_list_rate * (1 - discount_percentage / 100)`. - **`transaction_base.py::set_item_rate_and_discounts`** applies the same order when an item is selected: blanket order rate, then margin, then percentage discount, then discount amount.

final answer
The shared controller is `erpnext/controllers/taxes_and_totals.py`, which has the server-side `calculate_taxes_and_totals` class. The client-side `erpnext/public/js/controllers/taxes_and_totals.js` mirrors it. The current per-item rate logic is in `calculate_item_rate(item)` on the Python side. I read the code directly and did not run it. ## Call chain `calculate_taxes_and_totals.calculate()` calls `calculate_item_values()`. That function does the following: 1. It returns early if `is_consolidated` is set or `discount_amount_applied` is true. 2. For each item, it calls `round_floats_in(item)` and then `calculate_item_rate(item)`. 3. It sets `net_rate = rate`. 4. It sets `amount = rate * qty`. For returns and debit notes with qty 0, the amount is `-rate` or `rate` instead. 5. It sets `net_amount = amount`. 6. It calls `_set_in_company_currency` to fill the `base_*` fields. - These are `price_list_rate`, `rate_with_margin`, `rate`, `net_rate`, `amount` and `net_amount`. The later steps compute taxes and totals from these values. ## `calculate_item_rate(item)` 1. **No price list rate.** If `price_list_rate` is falsy, it calls `remove_margin` and `remove_discount`, sets `rate_with_margin = 0`, and returns. The rate is then whatever the user typed. 2. **Pricing rules.** `has_pricing_rules` is true when `item.pricing_rules` is set and `doc.ignore_pricing_rule` is not. - It calls `remove_margin(item)`. - It then loops over `get_applied_pricing_rules(item.pricing_rules)` and loads each rule with `frappe.get_cached_doc("Pricing Rule", ...)`. - A rule with a `margin_type` and `margin_rate_or_amount` is copied onto the item. This applies only if the type is "Percentage" or the rule currency matches the document currency. - Rule discounts are not applied here. They arrive earlier in `discount_percentage` or `discount_amount` through the pricing-rule and item-details flow, and this function just uses them. 3. **Margin.** `rate_with_margin = get_rate_with_margin(item)`. - With no `margin_type`, the result is `price_list_rate`. - "Percentage" gives `price_list_rate * (1 + m/100)`. - Otherwise it is `price_list_rate + m`. 4. **Discount.** If `discount_percentage > 0`, then `discount_amount = rate_with_margin * pct / 100`. Then `calculated_rate = rate_with_margin - discount_amount`, rounded to the rate precision. 5. **Choosing the rate.** - If pricing rules apply or `item.rate` is 0, `item.rate = calculated_rate`. - If `item.rate == calculated_rate`, nothing changes. - Otherwise the user's rate wins and margin and discount are reset to match it (see below). ## Manual rate override When `item.rate` differs from `calculated_rate` and no pricing rule applies, the user's rate is kept and the other fields are adjusted: - **Rate above `price_list_rate`:** - `margin_type` becomes "Amount". - `margin_rate_or_amount = rate - price_list_rate`. - `rate_with_margin = rate`. - `remove_discount` clears the discount. - **Rate at or below `price_list_rate`:** - `rate_with_margin = price_list_rate`. - `discount_amount = price_list_rate - rate`. - `discount_percentage = 0`. - `remove_margin` clears the margin. If pricing rules are active, the manual rate is not preserved. It gets overwritten by the calculated rate. ## Client-side equivalents - **`apply_pricing_rule_on_item(item)`** in `taxes_and_totals.js`: - It starts from `price_list_rate`, or from `blanket_order_rate` on Sales Order and Quotation. - It applies the margin (Percentage or Amount) to get `rate_with_margin`. - It derives `discount_amount` from `discount_percentage`. - It sets `rate = rate_with_margin - discount_amount` through `frappe.model.set_value`. - `transaction.js` calls it when a pricing rule is applied and when the margin type changes. - **The `"rate"` field handler** in `transaction.js` (`frappe.ui.form.on(<doctype> Item, "rate", …)`) handles a manual override in the browser. - It uses a JS `get_rate_with_margin`, which mirrors the Python helper of the same name. - It applies the same rule as the server. A rate above the price list rate becomes an Amount margin with the discount cleared. Otherwise the margin is cleared and a discount is set. - If there is no `price_list_rate`, or a `blanket_order_rate` is set, it zeroes the margin, discount and `rate_with_margin`. - It then recomputes `base_rate_with_margin` and calls `calculate_taxes_and_totals`. - **`calculate_item_values()`** in `taxes_and_totals.js` only sets `net_rate`, `amount`, `net_amount` and the `base_*` fields from the existing `rate`. It does not recompute the rate.

final answer
In the shared client-side controller `erpnext/public/js/controllers/taxes_and_totals.js` (`erpnext.taxes_and_totals`), the item rate is set in `apply_pricing_rule_on_item(item)`. The later totals functions only consume that rate. I read this controller and `transaction.js` directly. I did not trace the server-side Python. **1. Price list rate to rate: `apply_pricing_rule_on_item(item)`** - **Base rate:** it starts from `item.price_list_rate`. For Sales Order and Quotation rows that have a `blanket_order_rate`, it uses that instead. - **Margin:** it computes `rate_with_margin` from `margin_type` and `margin_rate_or_amount`. - If the margin type is Percentage, it is `base * (1 + margin_rate_or_amount / 100)`. - Otherwise it is `base + margin_rate_or_amount`. - The result is rounded to the `rate_with_margin` precision. - **Discount:** - If `discount_percentage` is set, `discount_amount` is recomputed as `rate_with_margin * discount_percentage / 100`. - If only `discount_amount` is set, it is used as given. - The discount is applied to the margin-inclusive rate. - **Rate:** `rate = rate_with_margin - discount_amount`, rounded to the `rate` precision. It is written with `frappe.model.set_value(doctype, name, "rate", item_rate)`, which fires the `rate` field event and a recalculation. - **Pricing rules:** the server-side pricing-rule logic (`erpnext/accounts/doctype/pricing_rule`) is what fills `price_list_rate`, `margin_type`, `margin_rate_or_amount`, `discount_percentage` and `discount_amount` on the row. I did not trace that code. `apply_pricing_rule_on_item` only reads those fields, so a pricing rule affects the rate only through them. **2. Triggers in `transaction.js`** - **`price_list_rate(doc, cdt, cdn)`:** it rounds the two fields and then calls `apply_pricing_rule_on_item`. The doctype check there is `in_list([...]), cdt` with a comma, so it always evaluates to `cdt` and is always truthy. The `else` branch, `rate = price_list_rate * (1 - discount_percentage / 100)`, is therefore effectively dead code. The method ends with `calculate_taxes_and_totals()`. - **`margin_rate_or_amount`:** it sits right after `price_list_rate` in `transaction.js`. I did not read its body. **3. Totals: `calculate_taxes_and_totals`** This runs `_calculate_taxes_and_totals` and then `calculate_discount_amount`. After those it handles shipping, advances, commission and the other document-level steps. - `_calculate_taxes_and_totals` runs `validate_conversion_rate`, `calculate_item_values`, `initialize_taxes`, `determine_exclusive_rate`, `calculate_net_total`, `calculate_taxes`, `adjust_grand_total_for_inclusive_tax`, `calculate_totals` and `_cleanup`. - `calculate_item_values` takes `rate` as its input. It sets `net_rate = rate` and `amount = net_amount = rate * qty`. Return and debit-note rows use an adjusted quantity. It then calls `set_in_company_currency` to fill the `base_*` fields (`base_price_list_rate`, `base_rate`, `base_amount`, `base_net_rate`, `base_net_amount`). - `determine_exclusive_rate` handles tax-inclusive prices. It backs the tax out of `amount` to get `net_amount` and `net_rate`. `rate` itself is not changed. **4. Manual rate override** - Nothing in `calculate_item_values` or `calculate_taxes_and_totals` recomputes `rate` from `price_list_rate`. The calculation is one-directional: `rate` is simply the input to `amount`. - If the user types a `rate`, the `rate` field handler runs `calculate_taxes_and_totals`. `amount` and `net_*` are rebuilt from the typed value, and the margin and discount fields are not re-applied. - The typed rate survives until something triggers `apply_pricing_rule_on_item` again, for example a change to `price_list_rate`, `discount_percentage` or margin. That call overwrites the manual value with the computed one. - I did not read the `rate` handler or the server-side validation in `erpnext/controllers`, so I can't say how they treat a manual rate beyond this.

final answer
The outstanding amount on each Overdue Payment row is set in `create_dunning()` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/sales_invoice/mapper.py` (line 570). `Dunning.validate()` then uses it to compute interest and totals. I read the code but did not run it or any tests. **How the row is built** 1. `create_dunning()` calls `get_mapped_doc` to map the Sales Invoice to a Dunning. Each Payment Schedule row becomes an Overdue Payment row, but only if `doc.outstanding > 0` and `due_date < today`. 2. The field map copies `name` into `payment_schedule` and `parent` into `sales_invoice`. Through the default mapping, the schedule row's own `outstanding` becomes the row's `outstanding`. 3. The `postprocess_dunning` callback then overrides that value, but only when the invoice has exactly one payment schedule row. It matches the Overdue Payment row to that schedule row by `payment_schedule` name, and the override depends on the currencies: - **Party account currency differs from invoice currency:** `row.outstanding = source.payment_schedule[0].outstanding`. - **Otherwise:** `row.outstanding = source.outstanding_amount`. 4. It then calls `target.validate()`. **Why the foreign-currency case works** - `Sales Invoice.outstanding_amount` is stored in the party (receivable) account currency. - The Overdue Payment row, the Dunning, and the interest calculation are all in the invoice's transaction currency. - If the invoice is in USD but posts to an INR receivable account, `outstanding_amount` is in INR. Copying it into a USD row would mix currencies. - The Payment Schedule's `outstanding` is kept in transaction currency and is reduced as payments are allocated. It therefore stays correct even if the invoice and its payments post at different exchange rates. A code comment in the function cites issue #56006 for this. - For invoices with several schedule rows, the override doesn't apply. Each row keeps its own schedule `outstanding`, which is already in transaction currency. **What `Dunning.validate()` does with it** (`erpnext/accounts/doctype/dunning/dunning.py`) - `validate_same_currency()` throws an error if any row's invoice currency differs from the Dunning's currency. - `validate_overdue_payments()` computes `overdue_days` from the posting date and due date. It then computes `interest = outstanding * (rate_of_interest/100/365) * overdue_days`. - `validate_totals()` sets `total_outstanding` as the sum of the row outstandings, and `dunning_amount` as total interest plus the dunning fee. It sets `base_dunning_amount` as `dunning_amount * conversion_rate`, and `grand_total` as `total_outstanding + dunning_amount`.

final answer
When a Dunning is created from an overdue Sales Invoice, each Overdue Payment row's `outstanding` is set in `create_dunning` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/sales_invoice/mapper.py`. I read that function but did not trace what `target.validate()` does afterwards, so any later recalculation on the Dunning is not covered here. **Which rows are created** - `get_mapped_doc` maps each `Payment Schedule` row of the invoice to an `Overdue Payment` row. - A schedule row is mapped only if `doc.outstanding > 0` and `getdate(doc.due_date) < getdate()`, so only overdue rows with something left to pay become rows. - The mapping copies the schedule row's `name` into `payment_schedule` and the invoice into `sales_invoice`. - Because the schedule row is the source, the row's `outstanding` starts as the schedule row's own `outstanding`. **How `outstanding` is computed** The inner function `postprocess_dunning` then adjusts it: - It only does this when the invoice has exactly one payment schedule row. It finds the Overdue Payment row whose `payment_schedule` matches that row's name. - If the invoice's `party_account_currency` is set and differs from `source.currency`, it sets `row.outstanding = source.payment_schedule[0].outstanding`. - Otherwise it sets `row.outstanding = source.get("outstanding_amount")`. - Then it calls `target.validate()`. **Why this works with a foreign-currency invoice on a different-currency receivable account** - `Sales Invoice.outstanding_amount` is stored in the party (receivable) account currency. - The Overdue Payment row is in the invoice's transaction currency. - When those two currencies differ, `outstanding_amount` would be in the wrong currency, so the code uses the payment schedule's `outstanding` instead. - That field is kept in transaction currency and is updated as payments are allocated. It therefore stays correct even when the invoice and its payments post at different exchange rates. The code comment cites issue #56006. - When the two currencies match, `outstanding_amount` is already in the right currency, so it is used directly. **Related code** `is_overdue` in `sales_invoice/services/status.py` uses the same currency idea. It sums `base_payment_amount` when `party_account_currency != currency`, and `payment_amount` otherwise.

final answer
When a Dunning is created from an overdue Sales Invoice, each Overdue Payment row's `outstanding` comes from the invoice's Payment Schedule row. In a foreign-currency invoice that posts to a receivable account in another currency, the row is read from the Payment Schedule's own `outstanding` rather than from the invoice's `outstanding_amount`. I read the code and the regression test but did not run the tests. **Where it happens:** `create_dunning()` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/sales_invoice/mapper.py` (about lines 570–618). It uses `get_mapped_doc` with a table map from "Payment Schedule" to "Overdue Payment". **Row selection and base values** - Only Payment Schedule rows with `outstanding > 0` and `getdate(due_date) < getdate()` are mapped to Overdue Payment rows. - `outstanding` and the other fields are copied from the Payment Schedule row by name. - `name` and `parent` are mapped to `payment_schedule` and `sales_invoice`. **Overriding `outstanding` in `postprocess_dunning`** - This only happens when the invoice has exactly one payment schedule row. The code finds the row whose `payment_schedule` matches `source.payment_schedule[0].name`. - If `source.party_account_currency` is set and differs from `source.currency`, it sets `row.outstanding = source.payment_schedule[0].outstanding`. - Otherwise it sets `row.outstanding = source.get("outstanding_amount")`. - Afterwards it calls `target.validate()`. **Why the foreign-currency branch is correct** - `Sales Invoice.outstanding_amount` is held in the party (receivable) account currency. - The Overdue Payment row is in the invoice's transaction currency, and the Dunning is validated as a single currency. - The Payment Schedule's `outstanding` stays in transaction currency and is updated as payments are allocated. It therefore stays correct even when the invoice and its payments post at different exchange rates (issue #56006). **Regression test:** `test_dunning_outstanding_uses_transaction_currency` in `test_dunning.py`. - A USD invoice at rate 100 with conversion rate 50 posts to an INR debtors account. - `si.outstanding_amount` is 5000, in INR. - `payment_schedule[0].outstanding` is 100, in USD. - The resulting `overdue_payments[0].outstanding` is 100, not 5000. **What happens afterwards in `Dunning.validate`** (`dunning.py`) - `validate_same_currency` checks that the invoice rows share one currency. - `validate_overdue_payments` computes each row's interest as `outstanding * daily_interest * overdue_days`. - `validate_totals` sets `total_outstanding` to the sum of the row `outstanding` values, `total_interest` to the sum of the row interest, and `grand_total` to `total_outstanding + dunning_amount`. - All of these therefore stay in transaction currency.

final answer
The code is in `create_dunning` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/sales_invoice/mapper.py` (line ~570). The "Fetch Overdue Payments" button in `dunning.js` calls it. **How the rows are built** - `get_mapped_doc` maps each Sales Invoice `Payment Schedule` row to an `Overdue Payment` row. The `payment_schedule` field gets the schedule row's name, and `sales_invoice` gets the parent invoice. - Only schedule rows that pass `doc.outstanding > 0 and getdate(doc.due_date) < getdate()` are mapped. Because `Overdue Payment` is a child of `Payment Schedule`, the `outstanding` and `due_date` fields carry over by matching field names. - `postprocess_dunning` then fills in the default Dunning Type's rate of interest, fee, income account and cost center. It also gets the letter text. **How the outstanding amount is set** - The post-processing step overrides the mapped value only when the invoice has exactly one payment schedule row. It finds the `Overdue Payment` row whose `payment_schedule` matches that schedule row. - By default it sets `row.outstanding = source.outstanding_amount`. - If `source.party_account_currency` is set and differs from `source.currency`, it instead sets `row.outstanding = source.payment_schedule[0].outstanding`. - With several schedule rows, the override is skipped. Each row keeps its own `Payment Schedule.outstanding`. - At the end, `target.validate()` runs. **Why the currency branch matters** - `Sales Invoice.outstanding_amount` is in the receivable account's currency (the party account currency). - The Dunning and its Overdue Payment rows are in the invoice's transaction currency. `Dunning.validate_same_currency` throws if the invoice currency differs from the dunning currency. - Copying `outstanding_amount` directly would therefore mix currencies when the invoice is, say, in USD but posts to an EUR receivable account. - The code's comment says the schedule's own `outstanding` is kept in transaction currency and updated as payments are allocated. It stays correct even when the invoice and its payments post at different exchange rates (#56006). I didn't check where that update happens. **Downstream calculation** `Dunning.validate` runs these steps, in `dunning.py`: - `validate_overdue_payments` sets `overdue_days = posting_date - due_date`. It then sets `interest = outstanding * (rate_of_interest/100/365) * overdue_days` for each row. - `validate_totals` sums `total_outstanding` and `total_interest`. It then sets `dunning_amount = total_interest + dunning_fee`, `base_dunning_amount = dunning_amount * conversion_rate`, and `grand_total = total_outstanding + dunning_amount`. All of these amounts stay in the invoice's transaction currency. Only `base_dunning_amount` is converted, using the dunning's `conversion_rate`.

The Item form loads several pieces of auxiliary data (attribute autocomplete, item price previews, opening stock creation) via whitelisted server calls. How does each of these guard against being called by a user who lacks the appropriate read/write permission?

final answer
All three calls are in `erpnext/stock/doctype/item/item.py`. Each is decorated with `@frappe.whitelist()` and does its own explicit permission check before doing any work. - **Attribute autocomplete: `get_item_attribute(parent, attribute_value="")`** - It starts with `if not frappe.has_permission("Item"): frappe.throw(_("No Permission"))`. - This is a doctype-level check with the default `ptype` of read. It doesn't check a specific document. - After that it runs `frappe.get_all("Item Attribute Value", ...)` with a `like` filter on `attribute_value`. - `get_all` doesn't apply permissions itself, so the explicit `Item` check is the only guard. The Item form calls this at `item.js:1483`. - **Item price preview: `get_item_prices(item_code)`** - It starts with `if not frappe.has_permission("Item Price", "read"): frappe.throw(_("Not permitted"), frappe.PermissionError)`. - It checks read permission on `Item Price`, not on `Item`, because the data comes from that doctype. - It then reads the prices with a `frappe.qb` query, which also skips permission handling. The query is limited to 11 rows, and it returns 10 plus a `has_more` flag. - The check is doctype-level only. It doesn't check access to the particular `item_code`. The Item form calls this at `item.js:861`. - **Opening stock creation: `make_opening_stock_entry(item_code, company, qty, valuation_rate, warehouse=None)`** - It starts with `if not frappe.has_permission("Item", "write", item_code): frappe.throw(_("Not permitted"), frappe.PermissionError)`. - This is the strictest of the three. It requires write permission on the specific Item document. - Only after that check does it run its business-rule validations: - The item must be a stock item. - It can't have serial or batch numbers. - No stock ledger entries may exist for it. - The quantity must be greater than 0. - The valuation rate can't be negative. - The warehouse must belong to the company. - A Temporary account must exist for the company. - It then calls `create_opening_stock_reconciliation(...)` to build the Stock Reconciliation. The Item form calls this at `item.js:1001`. - I only read the start of `create_opening_stock_reconciliation`, so I haven't confirmed whether it saves or submits with permissions ignored. Each guard raises an error (`frappe.throw`, or `PermissionError` in the last two). A caller without the permission gets an error rather than data.

final answer
All three endpoints are in `erpnext/stock/doctype/item/item.py`. Each is decorated with `@frappe.whitelist()` and runs an explicit `frappe.has_permission` check as its first statement. 1. **`get_item_attribute(parent, attribute_value="")`** is the attribute autocomplete for the child table. - It checks `frappe.has_permission("Item")`. With no ptype given, that defaults to read on the Item doctype. - If the check fails, it calls `frappe.throw(_("No Permission"))`. - After that it queries `Item Attribute Value` with `frappe.get_all`, which does not apply permission checks itself. The up-front check is therefore the only guard. - The check is on the Item doctype, not on Item Attribute. Item Attribute grants access only to the Item Manager role, so an Item reader who is not an Item Manager can still get these values. 2. **`get_item_prices(item_code)`** is the item price preview for the prices tab. - It checks `frappe.has_permission("Item Price", "read")`. - If the check fails, it throws `frappe.PermissionError` with "Not permitted". - It then reads with `frappe.qb`, which bypasses permission and user-permission filtering. Again the up-front check is the only guard. - The check is doctype-level on Item Price, not tied to the particular item. It also doesn't check read on the Item itself. - The query filters out cancelled records (`docstatus != 2`) and expired prices, and returns at most 10 rows plus a `has_more` flag. 3. **`make_opening_stock_entry(item_code, company, qty, valuation_rate, warehouse=None)`** creates the opening stock. - It checks `frappe.has_permission("Item", "write", item_code)`. This is a document-level write check on that specific item, so user permissions and sharing apply. - If the check fails, it throws `frappe.PermissionError`. - Only after the check does it load the item with `frappe.get_doc("Item", item_code)`. Then it validates the request: - The item must be a stock item. - It must not have serial or batch numbers. - No stock ledger entries may already exist for it. - `qty` must be greater than 0. - `valuation_rate` must not be negative. - If a warehouse is given, it must belong to the company. - I read the function only up to the warehouse validation (around line 1664). I didn't read the part that builds and submits the Stock Entry, so I haven't checked whether that part adds its own permission handling.

final answer
All three calls check permission at the top of the function. Two of them throw `frappe.PermissionError`, and the attribute autocomplete throws a plain error. The source is `erpnext/stock/doctype/item/item.py`. - **Attribute autocomplete: `get_item_attribute(parent, attribute_value="")`**, called from `item.js` around line 1483. - It runs `frappe.has_permission("Item")` first. With no `ptype`, Frappe checks the default `read` permission on the Item doctype. - If the user lacks it, the function calls `frappe.throw(_("No Permission"))`. This is a generic validation error, not a `frappe.PermissionError`. - Only after that does it run `frappe.get_all("Item Attribute Value", ...)` filtered by `parent` and a `LIKE %value%` match on `attribute_value`. - `frappe.get_all` ignores user permissions, so the check at the top is the only guard on this path. - **Item price preview: `get_item_prices(item_code)`**, called from `render_item_prices` in `item.js` at line 861. - It runs `frappe.has_permission("Item Price", "read")` first. It checks the Item Price doctype, not Item. - On failure it calls `frappe.throw(_("Not permitted"), frappe.PermissionError)`. - The query is a `frappe.qb` query, which does no permission filtering of its own. The doctype-level read check therefore protects it. - The check is doctype-level only. It doesn't check access to the particular `item_code`. - The query returns at most 11 rows, and `has_more` is set when 11 come back. - **Opening stock creation: `make_opening_stock_entry(item_code, company, qty, valuation_rate, warehouse=None)`**, called from the dialog in `item.js` at line 1001. - It runs `frappe.has_permission("Item", "write", item_code)` first. This is a document-level write check on the specific item. - On failure it calls `frappe.throw(_("Not permitted"), frappe.PermissionError)`. - After that check it validates the item: it must be a stock item, must not have serial or batch numbers, and must have no existing stock ledger entries. It also checks that `qty` is positive, `valuation_rate` is not negative, and the warehouse belongs to the company. - I read the function only as far as the opening-account lookup (about line 1665). I didn't read the part that creates the stock entry, so I can't say whether it ignores permissions when it saves. I didn't open the second opening-stock path, `Item.after_insert` and `set_opening_stock`, which runs when a new item is saved. It isn't a whitelisted call.
Q13Group C — Semantic gap (no keyword overlap with the code)In ERPNext, some stock sitting in a warehouse may already be promised to a specific customer. How does the system know that promis…
In ERPNext, some stock sitting in a warehouse may already be promised to a specific customer. How does the system know that promised goods cannot be used for someone else — where is the promise recorded, how is "still available to promise" calculated, and how does it stop an unrelated shipment from taking that stock? Name the relevant functions and files.

final answer
Reserved stock is recorded as submitted **Stock Reservation Entry** (SRE) documents. A running total is cached on the Bin. The Stock Ledger refuses any outgoing movement that would eat into that total. I read the code paths below but did not run anything. **Where the promise is recorded** - The doctype is `Stock Reservation Entry`, in `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_reservation_entry/stock_reservation_entry.py`. - Each entry holds the item, warehouse, voucher (usually a Sales Order and its line), `reserved_qty`, `delivered_qty`, `transferred_qty` and `consumed_qty`. It also holds `reservation_based_on` ("Qty" or "Serial and Batch") and, for serial/batch items, the specific serial and batch entries. - `validate_reservation_based_on_qty` and `validate_reservation_based_on_serial_and_batch` check an entry when it is saved. - `validate_with_allowed_qty` stops you reserving more than is available. - `on_submit` and `on_cancel` call `update_reserved_stock_in_bin`. - That method calls `Bin.update_reserved_stock` in `erpnext/stock/doctype/bin/bin.py`. It stores `get_sre_reserved_qty_for_item_and_warehouse(...)` in the Bin's `reserved_stock` field. - This is separate from the older `Bin.reserved_qty`, which comes from open Sales Orders and feeds `projected_qty`. **How "available to promise" is calculated** - Qty-based items use `get_available_qty_to_reserve(item_code, warehouse, batch_no, ignore_sre)` in the same SRE file. - It returns the stock balance (`get_stock_balance`) minus the sum of `reserved_qty - delivered_qty - transferred_qty - consumed_qty`, counting only submitted entries where `delivered_qty < reserved_qty`. - It takes `FOR UPDATE` locks on the rows it sums, so two concurrent reservations can't both claim the same stock. On Postgres it locks the Bin row first. - Batch items use `get_batch_qty` instead. - Serial-number items use `get_available_serial_nos_to_reserve`. It takes the available serial numbers and subtracts the ones already attached to submitted SREs. - The aggregate helpers are `get_sre_reserved_qty_for_item_and_warehouse` and `get_sre_reserved_qty_for_items_and_warehouses`. The per-voucher helpers are `get_sre_reserved_qty_details_for_voucher` and `get_sre_reserved_qty_for_voucher_detail_no`. - The Stock Balance and Reserved Stock reports read these. **How an unrelated shipment is blocked** - All of this is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. - `update_entries_after.__init__` calls `get_reserved_stock()`. That sums `reserved_qty` minus delivered, transferred and consumed quantities, for submitted SREs on that item and warehouse created up to the posting datetime. The result goes in `self.reserved_stock`. - When the ledger finds a negative-stock condition, it appends this to the error: "…units are reserved for other sales orders, you are allowed to consume only N units" (or "the full stock is reserved…"). The check is around line 1357 (`diff = qty_after_transaction + actual_qty - reserved_stock`) and the message is built near line 1860. - `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle` calls `validate_reserved_stock(args)` whenever `args.reserved_stock` is non-zero. It throws "Reserved Stock: N units … needed" if `get_stock_balance - reserved_stock` is below zero. - Serial and batch items are checked in `validate_reserved_serial_nos` and `validate_reserved_batch_nos`. They use `get_sre_reserved_serial_nos_details` and `get_sre_reserved_batch_nos_details` from the SRE module. They throw "Serial Nos are reserved in Stock Reservation Entries, you need to unreserve them…" or "Reserved Stock for Batch". **How the promise is released** - When the reserving customer's own Delivery Note posts, the entry's `delivered_qty` goes up. The entry then drops out of the sums, because those only count entries where `delivered_qty < reserved_qty`. - A reservation can also be consumed or transferred, which raises `consumed_qty` or `transferred_qty`. - Cancelling the SRE triggers `update_status` and a Bin update.

final answer
Stock that is promised to a customer is recorded as a **Stock Reservation Entry** (SRE). ERPNext then subtracts the open SRE quantity from the stock balance whenever it checks availability or posts a stock movement. I read the code paths below but did not run any of this. **Where the promise is recorded** - The record is the `Stock Reservation Entry` doctype, in `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_reservation_entry/stock_reservation_entry.py`. - Each SRE holds the item, warehouse and `reserved_qty`. It also holds the progress fields `delivered_qty`, `transferred_qty` and `consumed_qty`, and optionally serial or batch numbers through a serial and batch bundle. - The SRE links back to the voucher that made the promise. For a customer promise this is the Sales Order line, through `voucher_type`, `voucher_no` and `voucher_detail_no`. - Sales Order items are reserved through `create_stock_reservation_entries_for_so_items`. Work Orders, Subcontracting Orders and Production Plans use `StockReservation.make_stock_reservation_entries` and `transfer_reservation_entries_to`. - On submit, cancel or update, `StockReservationEntry.update_reserved_stock_in_bin` calls `Bin.update_reserved_stock` (`erpnext/stock/doctype/bin/bin.py`). That function sums the open reservations with `get_sre_reserved_qty_for_item_and_warehouse` and stores the result in `Bin.reserved_stock`. **How "available to promise" is calculated** - `get_available_qty_to_reserve(item_code, warehouse, batch_no, ignore_sre)` is the module-level function. `StockReservation.get_available_qty_to_reserve` is the class version of the same calculation. - For a batch, it returns `get_batch_qty(...)`. - Otherwise it takes `get_stock_balance(item, warehouse)` from `erpnext/stock/utils.py`. That balance is the latest `qty_after_transaction` in the Stock Ledger Entry. - It then subtracts `Sum(reserved_qty − delivered_qty − transferred_qty − consumed_qty)` over submitted SREs for that item and warehouse that are not fully delivered (`delivered_qty < reserved_qty`). - The query locks the rows with `FOR UPDATE`, so two concurrent reservations can't both claim the same stock. - `StockReservationEntry.validate_with_allowed_qty` uses this figure to refuse a reservation larger than what is free. A separate check, `validate_reservation_based_on_serial_and_batch`, handles serial and batch reservations. **How an unrelated shipment is stopped** - **Ledger posting, `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`:** - The ledger-posting class (in `stock_ledger.py`) sets `self.reserved_stock = self.get_reserved_stock()` when it is created. This is the total of submitted SRE quantity still open for that item and warehouse, counting SREs created up to the posting time. - The negative-stock check includes the reserved amount: `diff = qty_after_transaction + actual_qty − reserved_stock`. If the result is negative, the posting fails. - The error message then adds "As N units are reserved for other sales orders, you are allowed to consume only X units", or "the full stock is reserved" if nothing is free. - The validation function (around line 2399 of `stock_ledger.py`) also calls `validate_reserved_stock(args)` when `reserved_stock` is set. It recomputes `get_stock_balance − reserved_stock` and throws a "Reserved Stock" error if that is negative. - **Serial and batch items:** - `validate_reserved_serial_nos` and `validate_reserved_batch_nos` in `stock_ledger.py` reject a serial or batch number that an SRE has reserved. They use `get_sre_reserved_serial_nos_details` and `get_sre_reserved_batch_nos_details`. - The error tells the user to unreserve first. - **The owning voucher is allowed through:** - The voucher that owns the reservation consumes it. Delivery Notes update `delivered_qty` through `update_delivered_qty` and `update_reserved_qty_in_voucher`. - Work Order and Subcontracting Order stock entries consume it. `StockEntrySABB.get_available_reserved_materials` in `stock_entry/services/serial_batch.py` calls `get_reserved_materials` to pick up their reserved serial and batch numbers. - Once `delivered_qty`, `transferred_qty` or `consumed_qty` rises, the open reservation shrinks and that stock becomes available again.

final answer
The promise is recorded as a **Stock Reservation Entry** (SRE). "Available to promise" is actual stock minus the open reservations. An unrelated shipment is blocked by a check in the stock ledger. I read these functions but did not run any of this. **Where the promise is recorded** - The doctype is `Stock Reservation Entry`, in `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_reservation_entry/stock_reservation_entry.py`. - Each entry holds the item, warehouse, `reserved_qty`, the voucher it serves (such as a Sales Order) and the status. - It also tracks how much has been used so far: `delivered_qty`, `transferred_qty` and `consumed_qty`. - It can reserve by quantity or by specific serial/batch numbers through a serial and batch bundle. - `StockReservation.make_stock_reservation_entries()` creates the entries, and `cancel_stock_reservation_entries()` cancels them. - The entry's `on_submit` calls `update_reserved_stock_in_bin()`, which calls `Bin.update_reserved_stock()`. This stores the running total in the `reserved_stock` field on the Bin (the item/warehouse record). - `update_status()` moves the entry through Reserved, Partially Reserved, Partially Delivered, Delivered, Closed and Cancelled. - `update_delivered_qty()` records deliveries against the entry, so the promise is consumed when the goods ship to the intended customer. **How "still available to promise" is calculated** - `get_available_qty_to_reserve(item_code, warehouse, batch_no, ignore_sre)` is the main function (line 693). - Without a batch, it takes `get_stock_balance(item_code, warehouse)`. It then subtracts the sum of `reserved_qty - delivered_qty - transferred_qty - consumed_qty` over submitted SREs for that item and warehouse that are not fully delivered. - With a batch, it uses `get_batch_qty` instead. - It locks the rows it sums with `for_update`, using the Bin row on Postgres, so two concurrent reservations can't both claim the same stock. - `validate_with_allowed_qty()` and `validate_reservation_based_on_qty()` use this limit when a new reservation is made. - Serial numbers are handled by `get_available_serial_nos_to_reserve()`. - Helper functions give the reserved totals: `get_sre_reserved_qty_for_item_and_warehouse`, `get_sre_reserved_qty_for_items_and_warehouses`, `get_sre_reserved_qty_details_for_voucher`, `get_sre_reserved_serial_nos_details` and `get_sre_reserved_batch_nos_details`. **How an unrelated shipment is stopped** - This happens in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`, while stock ledger entries are being posted. - `update_entries_after.get_reserved_stock()` (about line 631) works out the open reserved quantity at the posting time. It is `Sum(reserved_qty) - (delivered + transferred + consumed)` for submitted SREs created on or before that time. - `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle()` (line 2355) calls `validate_reserved_stock(args)` when `args.reserved_stock` is set. - `validate_reserved_stock()` (line 2470) computes `balance_qty - reserved_stock`. If the result is negative, it throws "N units of Item needed in Warehouse … to complete this transaction" (titled "Reserved Stock"). So an unrelated issue or delivery that would dip into reserved stock is rejected. - For serial and batch items there are two further checks: - `validate_reserved_serial_nos()` throws "Serial Nos are reserved in Stock Reservation Entries, you need to unreserve them" if a reserved serial number is used. - `validate_reserved_batch_nos()` compares each batch's available quantity with its reserved quantity. - The reserving voucher itself is not blocked. When it delivers against its own reservation, `delivered_qty` rises, which lowers the open reserved amount. **Reporting** - The "Reserved Stock" report is in `erpnext/stock/report/reserved_stock/reserved_stock.py`, with `execute()` and `get_data()`.

final answer
Reserved stock in ERPNext is recorded in a document, summed against the warehouse balance to get what is still available, and enforced by a check in the stock ledger. I read most of this from the source. I did not open `bin.py` or `stock_reconciliation.py`, so the notes on them come from the code graph's call information only. **1. Where the promise is recorded** - The promise is a submitted **Stock Reservation Entry** (SRE). The code is in `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_reservation_entry/stock_reservation_entry.py`. - Each SRE holds an item, a warehouse, an optional batch or serial numbers, a `reserved_qty`, and the voucher it is for. That voucher is usually a Sales Order item, but can also be a Work Order or Production Plan. - Its progress is tracked in `delivered_qty`, `transferred_qty` and `consumed_qty`. - Sales Orders create SREs through `SalesOrderStockReservation.create_stock_reservation_entries` in `erpnext/selling/doctype/sales_order/services/reservation.py`. That calls `create_stock_reservation_entries_for_so_items`. Packed items go through `_reserve_packed_items`. - Work Orders and Production Plans use the `StockReservation` class, mainly `make_stock_reservation_entries`. - `has_unreserved_stock` and `get_unreserved_qty` work out how much of a Sales Order is not yet reserved. The formula is `stock_qty − delivered_qty × conversion_factor − already reserved`. - `validate_reserved_stock` in that same file only clears the `reserve_stock` flag on non-stock items or when Stock Settings has reservation turned off. - Whenever an SRE changes, `update_reserved_stock_in_bin` calls `Bin.update_reserved_stock` (`erpnext/stock/doctype/bin/bin.py:217`). That recomputes the Bin's `reserved_stock` field from the SREs through `get_sre_reserved_qty_for_item_and_warehouse`. **2. How "available to promise" is calculated** - The module-level `get_available_qty_to_reserve(item_code, warehouse, batch_no, ignore_sre)` is at line 693. It returns actual stock minus open reservations. - The actual stock is `get_stock_balance(item_code, warehouse)`. - The open reservations are the sum of `reserved_qty − delivered_qty − transferred_qty − consumed_qty` over submitted SREs (`docstatus == 1`) for that item and warehouse where `delivered_qty < reserved_qty`. - With a batch, it returns `get_batch_qty(...)` for that batch instead. It does this to avoid double-counting. - `ignore_sre` excludes one reservation, so an SRE being edited does not count against itself. - The aggregate query uses `for_update()` so two concurrent reservations cannot both claim the same stock. On Postgres it first locks the Bin row. - `StockReservation.get_available_qty_to_reserve` at line 1270 is a method version of the same calculation, without the locks. It is used by `make_stock_reservation_entries`. If nothing is available it calls `throw_stock_not_exists_error`. Otherwise it reserves the smaller of the requested and available quantities. **3. How an unrelated shipment is blocked** - The check is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle(args, ...)` first runs the normal negative-stock checks. - It then runs `if args.reserved_stock: validate_reserved_stock(args)`. This is the point where reserved stock is enforced, and it only runs when the transaction carries a `reserved_stock` value. - `validate_reserved_stock` (line 2470) takes `balance_qty = get_stock_balance(item, warehouse)` and computes `diff = balance_qty − reserved_stock`. If `diff` is negative beyond a 0.0001 tolerance, it throws "N units of X needed in Y … to complete this transaction" with the title "Reserved Stock". - In effect, an issue or delivery that is not the reserving voucher cannot take the balance below the reserved quantity. - The reserving voucher itself can deliver, because delivering increases `delivered_qty` and so lowers the open reservation. - `StockReconciliation` has its own `validate_reserved_stock` (`erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_reconciliation/stock_reconciliation.py:829`) so a reconciliation cannot cut stock below what is reserved. I did not open that file. - For serial-number reservations there is a separate check, `validate_reserved_serial_nos`, next to `validate_reserved_stock` in `stock_ledger.py`. I did not read its body.

When item prices in ERPNext already include tax, how does the system work backwards to determine the pre-tax amount of each line? Explain where this happens and how it handles multiple inclusive taxes, compounding tax rows, per-quantity taxes and deduction-type taxes.

final answer
Back-calculation of the pre-tax amount happens in `determine_exclusive_rate()` in `erpnext/controllers/taxes_and_totals.py`. It sits in the `calculate_taxes_and_totals` class. `_calculate()` calls it at line 84, before the net totals and the tax rows are computed. **Flow** - It returns immediately if no tax row has `included_in_print_rate` set. - Otherwise it loops over each item and loads that item's `item_tax_rate` JSON with `_load_item_tax_rate`. - For each tax row, `get_current_tax_fraction(tax, item_tax_map)` returns two values. One is a fractional rate contribution (`tax_fraction_for_current_item`). The other is a fixed amount per unit of quantity (`inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty`). - The rate comes from `_get_tax_rate`. An item-specific rate keyed by `account_head` in `item_tax_rate` overrides `tax.rate`. If the item's rate is `NOT_APPLICABLE_TAX`, the row contributes nothing for that item. **Final formula (lines 329–343)** - `amount = item.amount − Σ(inclusive per-qty amount × qty)` - `net_amount = amount / (1 + Σ tax_fractions)` - The result is stored unrounded in `item._unrounded_net_amount`. - `net_amount` and `net_rate = net_amount / qty` are then rounded to field precision. - The company-currency fields are set with `_set_in_company_currency`. - This is skipped if `discount_amount_applied` is set or if `qty` is 0. In the discount case, `_calculate` re-runs it after the discount is applied. **Multiple inclusive taxes** - Each inclusive row adds its fraction to `cumulated_tax_fraction`. For example, two inclusive rows "On Net Total" at 5% and 10% give a divisor of 1.15. - Rows that are not inclusive contribute 0, so they are computed normally later, on top of the net amount. **Compounding rows** - "On Net Total" uses `rate/100`. - "On Previous Row Amount" uses `rate/100 × (the referenced row's tax_fraction_for_current_item)`. The referenced row is `taxes[row_id − 1]`. - "On Previous Row Total" uses `rate/100 × (the referenced row's grand_total_fraction_for_current_item)`. - `grand_total_fraction_for_current_item` is a running total. Row 0 gets `1 + fraction`. Later rows get the previous row's value plus their own fraction. It mirrors the cumulative "total" column of the tax table. **Per-quantity taxes** - "On Item Quantity" contributes no fraction. It sets `inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty = tax_rate`, a fixed amount per unit. - It is multiplied by `item.qty` and subtracted from the gross amount before dividing by `1 + fractions`. The fixed amount is therefore removed first, and the percentage taxes are backed out of what remains. **Deduction taxes** - If `add_deduct_tax == "Deduct"`, both the fraction and the per-qty amount are multiplied by −1. - A deduction therefore lowers the divisor (or adds back the fixed amount). Inclusive deductions such as withholding tax are handled this way. **Later steps** - The tax-row calculation then runs over the net amounts. Around line 611 it checks `included_in_print_rate`, and near lines 702–707 it handles the rounding difference and adjustments for inclusive taxes. I did not read those later sections in detail.

final answer
**Where it happens** The back-calculation is in `calculate_taxes_and_totals.determine_exclusive_rate()` in `erpnext/controllers/taxes_and_totals.py` (lines 303–343). Its helper is `get_current_tax_fraction()` (348–382). The client has a mirror in `erpnext/public/js/controllers/taxes_and_totals.js` (`determine_exclusive_rate`, `get_current_tax_fraction`). `_calculate` runs it before `calculate_taxes`. If no tax row has `included_in_print_rate`, it returns immediately. **Per-item algorithm** For each item, it loads the item-specific tax map with `_load_item_tax_rate(item.item_tax_rate)`. It then walks the tax rows in order, with these running values: - Each row gets a `tax_fraction_for_current_item` and an `inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty` from `get_current_tax_fraction`. - `grand_total_fraction_for_current_item` is cumulative. Row 0 is `1 + fraction`. Each later row is the previous row's value plus its own fraction. - `cumulated_tax_fraction` is the sum of all row fractions. - `total_inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty` is the sum of `per_qty * item.qty`. The pre-tax amount is then: ``` amount = item.amount - total_inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty _unrounded_net_amount = amount / (1 + cumulated_tax_fraction) item.net_amount = round(unrounded) item.net_rate = net_amount / qty ``` This is skipped when `discount_amount_applied` is set or `qty` is 0. `_unrounded_net_amount` is kept so that `get_current_tax_and_net_amount` can compute inclusive "On Net Total" tax from the unrounded net. That avoids rounding twice. **Multiple inclusive taxes** Only rows with `included_in_print_rate` contribute a fraction. Their fractions add up in `cumulated_tax_fraction`. For example, two inclusive rows at 5% and 3% on net total give net = amount / 1.08. Rows that aren't inclusive give a fraction of 0. **Per-row handling in `get_current_tax_fraction`** - **On Net Total:** the fraction is `rate/100`. - **On Previous Row Amount (compounding):** the fraction is `rate/100 * taxes[row_id-1].tax_fraction_for_current_item`. It is a percentage of the earlier row's tax, not of net. - **On Previous Row Total (compounding):** the fraction is `rate/100 * taxes[row_id-1].grand_total_fraction_for_current_item`. That cumulative value is `1 + all earlier fractions`, so the row taxes net plus the earlier taxes. - **On Item Quantity (per-qty):** the fraction stays 0. The rate is returned as `inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty`. It is multiplied by `qty` and subtracted from the amount before dividing, because it is a fixed amount rather than a percentage. - **Actual:** it contributes nothing. - **Item tax rate overrides:** `_get_tax_rate` uses the item's tax rate for that account head if there is one, otherwise `tax.rate`. If the item's rate is `NOT_APPLICABLE_TAX`, the row returns 0 and 0. **Deduction taxes** If `tax.add_deduct_tax == "Deduct"`, both the fraction and the per-qty amount are multiplied by -1. A deducted inclusive tax therefore reduces the divisor, or is added back in the per-qty case. The `grand_total_fraction_for_current_item` chain then carries the negative fraction into later compounding rows. In the forward pass, `get_tax_amount_if_for_valuation_or_deduction` flips the sign for Deduct rows on purchase documents. It also zeroes the amount for Valuation-category rows. `set_item_wise_tax` applies a -1 multiplier for Deduct rows too. **After the back-calculation** `calculate_taxes` recomputes each tax from the new `net_amount`, using `get_current_tax_and_net_amount`. Its handling of each charge type is: - **On Net Total:** `rate/100 * net`, using the unrounded net for inclusive rows. - **On Previous Row Amount / Total:** `rate/100 * ` the previous row's per-item tax amount or running total. - **On Item Quantity:** `rate * qty`. The tax amounts it produces match the inclusive amount that was backed out.

final answer
The back-calculation happens in `determine_exclusive_rate()` in `erpnext/controllers/taxes_and_totals.py`. It is a method of the `calculate_taxes_and_totals` class. I read the code but did not run it. There is also a JS twin in `erpnext/public/js/controllers/taxes_and_totals.js`, which I did not open. ## Where it sits in the flow `_calculate()` runs these steps in order: 1. `calculate_item_values` 2. `update_item_tax_map` 3. `initialize_taxes` 4. **`determine_exclusive_rate`** 5. `calculate_net_total` 6. `calculate_taxes` 7. `adjust_grand_total_for_inclusive_tax` 8. `calculate_totals` `determine_exclusive_rate` returns immediately unless at least one tax row has `included_in_print_rate` set. When one does, it works out `net_amount` and `net_rate` for every item. Steps 5 to 8 then run on those net values. ## Per-item algorithm For each item, the method loads the item's `item_tax_rate` JSON through `_load_item_tax_rate`. It then loops over all tax rows and calls `get_current_tax_fraction(tax, item_tax_map)`. That call returns two values: a tax fraction and an inclusive amount per quantity. The method accumulates three things across the rows: - **`cumulated_tax_fraction`:** the sum of every row's `tax_fraction_for_current_item`. - **`total_inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty`:** the sum of each row's per-quantity amount multiplied by `item.qty`. - **`grand_total_fraction_for_current_item`:** a running per-row value. Row 0 gets `1 + fraction`. Later rows get the previous row's value plus their own fraction. Then, as long as no document-level discount has been applied yet (`discount_amount_applied` is false), `item.qty` is non-zero, and there is some inclusive tax: ``` amount = item.amount - total_inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty _unrounded_net_amount = amount / (1 + cumulated_tax_fraction) net_amount = round(_unrounded_net_amount) net_rate = net_amount / qty ``` The company-currency fields are then set with `_set_in_company_currency`. ## Each case **Multiple inclusive taxes.** The fractions of all inclusive rows add up in `cumulated_tax_fraction`. The gross amount is divided once by `1 + Σ fractions`, not once per tax. Rows that are not inclusive return a fraction of 0, so they don't affect the division. **Compounding rows.** In `get_current_tax_fraction`, each charge type gives a different fraction: - *On Net Total:* the fraction is `rate/100`. - *On Previous Row Amount:* the fraction is `rate/100` times the referenced row's `tax_fraction_for_current_item`. The referenced row is `taxes[row_id-1]`. - *On Previous Row Total:* the fraction is `rate/100` times the referenced row's `grand_total_fraction_for_current_item`, which is `1 + the fractions up to that row`. This is why a compounded tax's base includes the earlier taxes. **Per-quantity taxes (On Item Quantity).** These taxes don't scale with price. The fraction stays 0 and the method returns `inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty = rate`. `determine_exclusive_rate` multiplies that by `qty` and subtracts it from the gross amount before the division. The later `calculate_taxes` step computes this tax as `rate * qty`. It records no taxable amount for these rows, so `taxable_amount` is 0. **Deduction taxes.** If `add_deduct_tax == "Deduct"`, both the fraction and the per-quantity amount are multiplied by -1. A deducted inclusive tax therefore raises the net amount, because it makes `1 + Σ fractions` smaller. Later, `get_tax_amount_if_for_valuation_or_deduction` flips the sign of the tax amount, but only on purchase documents (Purchase Order, Purchase Invoice, Purchase Receipt, Supplier Quotation). `set_item_wise_tax` also applies a -1 multiplier to Deduct rows in the item-wise breakup. **Item-specific rates.** `_get_tax_rate` first looks for an override in the item's tax map, keyed by `account_head`. Otherwise it uses `tax.rate`. A `NOT_APPLICABLE_TAX` marker makes the row contribute nothing. ## Downstream consistency - In `get_current_tax_and_net_amount`, an inclusive On Net Total row uses `item._unrounded_net_amount` instead of the rounded `net_amount`. This avoids rounding twice. The other charge types use the rounded `net_amount` or the previous-row values as usual. - `adjust_grand_total_for_inclusive_tax` handles the remaining rounding difference. It compares `doc.total` plus the non-inclusive taxes against `last_tax.total`. If the gap is within half a unit of the tax precision, it stores the gap in `grand_total_diff`, and `calculate_totals` adds that to `grand_total`. A larger gap is ignored. - `set_item_wise_tax` spreads rounding error across items by taking each item's amount as the change in a running cumulative total. This makes the item-wise tax amounts sum exactly to the tax row's amount. `adjust_rounding_in_item_wise_tax_details` then puts any small leftover on the last detail row.

final answer
**Where it happens** The work is done in `calculate_taxes_and_totals.determine_exclusive_rate()` in `erpnext/controllers/taxes_and_totals.py`, around line 303. The pipeline calls it right after `initialize_taxes()` and before `calculate_net_total()` and `calculate_taxes()`. The client has a mirror of the same logic in `erpnext/public/js/controllers/taxes_and_totals.js`, in `determine_exclusive_rate` and `get_current_tax_fraction`. `PaymentEntry.determine_exclusive_rate` in `payment_entry.py` has a simplified version for `included_in_paid_amount`. **Algorithm** 1. The method returns early unless at least one tax row has `included_in_print_rate`. 2. For each item it loads the item-specific rates with `_load_item_tax_rate(item.item_tax_rate)`, which parses a JSON map of `account_head` to rate. 3. For each tax row it calls `get_current_tax_fraction(tax, item_tax_map)`. That returns two values: - `tax_fraction_for_current_item`, the tax as a fraction of the net amount. - `inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty`, a fixed amount per unit of quantity. 4. It sums the fractions into `cumulated_tax_fraction`. It also sums `inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty * item.qty` into `total_inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty`. 5. It then solves for the net amount, as long as no document-level discount has been applied (`discount_amount_applied`), the item has a quantity, and at least one of the two totals is non-zero: - `net = (item.amount − total_inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty) / (1 + cumulated_tax_fraction)` - The unrounded value is kept in `item._unrounded_net_amount`. - `net_amount` is that value rounded to precision, and `net_rate = net_amount / qty`. - The company-currency versions are set with `_set_in_company_currency`. **How each case is handled** - **Multiple inclusive taxes.** Each inclusive row adds its fraction to `cumulated_tax_fraction`. For example, two taxes at 10% and 5% on net total give a divisor of 1.15. Rows that are not marked `included_in_print_rate` contribute 0. A rate of `NOT_APPLICABLE_TAX` in the item tax map also contributes 0, and `_get_tax_rate` lets a per-item rate override `tax.rate`. - **Compounding rows.** The fraction depends on the charge type: - `On Net Total` gives `rate/100`. - `On Previous Row Amount` gives `rate/100 × (the referenced row's tax_fraction_for_current_item)`. - `On Previous Row Total` gives `rate/100 × (the referenced row's grand_total_fraction_for_current_item)`. - The referenced row is found with `row_id − 1`. - `grand_total_fraction_for_current_item` is a running total. Row 0 is `1 + its fraction`. Each later row is the previous row's value plus its own fraction. This is how "previous row total" compounding is expressed algebraically. - **Per-quantity taxes.** For `On Item Quantity`, the fraction stays 0 and `inclusive_tax_amount_per_qty = tax_rate`. The method multiplies it by `qty` and subtracts it from the line amount before dividing by `1 + fractions`. - **Deduction taxes.** If `add_deduct_tax == "Deduct"`, both the fraction and the per-quantity amount are multiplied by −1. A deducted inclusive tax therefore lowers the divisor, or adds back to the amount, instead of raising it. **Afterwards** - In `get_current_tax_and_net_amount`, the "On Net Total" branch uses `item._unrounded_net_amount` for inclusive taxes. This avoids double rounding when the tax amount is computed. - The "On Item Quantity" branch computes `tax_rate * qty`. - `adjust_grand_total_for_inclusive_tax` then handles leftover rounding differences in the grand total.

Trace both the receipt side and the billing side, naming the relevant functions and files.

final answer
ERPNext parks the value in the **Stock Received But Not Billed (SRBNB)** account. This is a liability account, set per company as `stock_received_but_not_billed`. For fixed assets the equivalent is **Asset Received But Not Billed**. I read the receipt-side and billing-side code below. I did not open `make_item_gl_entries` (the PI loop that posts the debit), so that step is inferred from `ExpenseAccountService` and the SRBNB handling I did read. ## Receipt side (Purchase Receipt) - `PurchaseReceipt.get_gl_entries()` in `erpnext/stock/doctype/purchase_receipt/purchase_receipt.py` delegates to `PurchaseReceiptGLComposer.compose()` in `erpnext/stock/doctype/purchase_receipt/services/gl_composer.py`. - For each stock item, with perpetual inventory on and a non-zero valuation rate, it looks up `stock_asset_rbnb`. That is the company default `stock_received_but_not_billed`, or `asset_received_but_not_billed` for fixed assets. - It then calls these inner helpers: - `make_item_asset_inward_gl_entry` debits the warehouse's stock/inventory account by the stock value difference. That difference comes from `get_stock_value_difference`. - `make_stock_received_but_not_billed_entry` credits SRBNB with the item's `base_net_amount`. The code writes this as a negative debit. - `make_landed_cost_gl_entries`, `make_expenses_added_to_stock_entries`, `make_amount_difference_entry`, `make_sub_contracting_gl_entries` and `make_divisional_loss_gl_entry` handle landed cost, valuation-tax expenses, price differences, subcontracting and rounding or variance. - Net effect: Dr Stock in Hand, Cr SRBNB. The value stays in SRBNB until the bill arrives. - If an item has a provisional expense account, the receipt uses `add_provisional_gl_entry` (`ProvisionalAccountingService`) instead. It credits that provisional account rather than SRBNB. ## Billing side (Purchase Invoice) - `ExpenseAccountService.set_expense_account()` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/expense_account.py` runs at validate. For a stock item without `update_stock`, it sets `item.expense_account` to SRBNB. It does this when SRBNB was credited in the linked Purchase Receipt. It also does it when there is no receipt, which covers invoice-before-receipt. - For fixed assets it uses the ARBNB account if the receipt booked it. Otherwise it uses the CWIP or fixed asset account. - The invoice's GL composer, `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer.compose()` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`, posts the entries. - `make_supplier_gl_entry` credits Creditors (the payable). - `make_item_gl_entries` posts the item debit to `item.expense_account`, which is SRBNB. This offsets the receipt's credit, so SRBNB nets to zero and the liability moves to Accounts Payable. - Where a receipt is linked and item taxes or valuation charges exist, `make_item_gl_entries` also debits SRBNB for `item_tax_amount`. It does this only if no valuation-tax entry was already booked in the receipt. It tracks the amount in `negative_expense_to_be_booked`. - `make_stock_adjustment_entry` posts any difference between the billed amount and the stock value booked in the receipt. - If the receipt used a provisional account, `make_provisional_gl_entry` and `get_provisional_accounts` reverse that account. - Billing status on the receipt is updated through `PurchaseReceipt.update_billing_status()`, which calls `BillingStatusService`.

final answer
Short answer: ERPNext parks the value in the **Stock Received But Not Billed** account (SRBNB). It is a company default account of type "Stock Received But Not Billed", and it is a liability-type clearing account. Receiving goods credits SRBNB. Booking the supplier's bill debits it again. I read the code paths below. I did not run any scenario. ## Receipt side (Purchase Receipt) The Purchase Receipt GL entries are built in `erpnext/stock/doctype/purchase_receipt/services/gl_composer.py`, in `PurchaseReceiptGLComposer._make_item_gl_entries`. It only runs when perpetual inventory is enabled and the item is a stock item. For each item row, it picks the SRBNB account: - A normal item uses `doc.get_company_default("stock_received_but_not_billed")`. - A fixed-asset item uses `asset_received_but_not_billed`, the Asset Received But Not Billed account. This is the parallel holding account for assets. It then calls these helpers in order: 1. **`make_item_asset_inward_gl_entry`** debits the warehouse's Stock account (or the asset account) by `stock_value_diff`. The amount comes from `get_stock_value_difference()` in `purchase_receipt.py`, which reads the Stock Ledger Entry for that receipt row. `against_account` is set to SRBNB. 2. **`make_stock_received_but_not_billed_entry`** books the credit to SRBNB for the row's `base_net_amount`. In the code it is written as `debit=-1*outgoing_amount`, a negative debit that works out to a credit. This is the "parking" step. - For internal transfers and rejected-warehouse stock the amount comes from the stock value difference instead. - If the Purchase Receipt row was already billed through a Purchase Invoice and the exchange rate differs, it adds a credit to SRBNB and a debit to the exchange gain/loss account. 3. **`make_landed_cost_gl_entries`**, **`make_expenses_added_to_stock_entries`**, **`make_amount_difference_entry`** and **`make_sub_contracting_gl_entries`** add the other legs. These cover landed cost, expenses included in valuation, differences from the invoice, and subcontracting raw material cost. The net effect is Dr Stock (inventory) and Cr SRBNB. No payable to the supplier exists yet. The "Received Items To Be Billed" report (`accounts/report/received_items_to_be_billed`) lists the receipt rows where `billed_amt < amount`. These are the goods still waiting in the holding account. ## Billing side (Purchase Invoice created against the receipt, with Update Stock off) **1. Choosing the expense account.** `ExpenseAccountService.set_expense_account` in `accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/expense_account.py` runs during validation. With perpetual inventory on and a stock item, it forces `item.expense_account` to the SRBNB account instead of whatever the user entered. - If the linked Purchase Receipt already has a GL Entry on SRBNB, it sets the account to SRBNB and shows the "Expense Head changed" message. - If there is no receipt at all, it also uses SRBNB. This handles a bill that arrives before the goods. In that case the invoice itself parks the value there, and a later receipt clears it. - With `update_stock` on, the invoice instead uses the warehouse's Stock account directly. It does not go through SRBNB. **2. Posting the entries.** `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer` is in `accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`. It is called from `PurchaseInvoice.get_gl_entries`. - **`make_supplier_gl_entry`** credits the Payable account with the supplier's bill. - **`make_item_gl_entries`** has an else branch for a stock item when `update_stock` is off. That branch debits `item.expense_account`, which is now SRBNB, for `base_net_amount`, with `against` set to the supplier. This debit cancels the credit from the receipt. A comment there says the receipt already booked the stock and any Purchase Price Variance, so the invoice only clears SRBNB at the full billed amount. - After that debit it checks whether the exchange rate changed between receipt and invoice. If the rate differs, it books the difference to exchange gain/loss. It skips this when "set landed cost based on purchase invoice rate" is on. The result is Dr SRBNB and Cr Payable. Combined with the receipt's Dr Stock and Cr SRBNB, the net is Dr Stock and Cr Payable, and SRBNB returns to zero. ## Linking the two documents The invoice row carries `purchase_receipt` and `pr_detail`. The receipt row's `billed_amt` is updated, which makes the item drop out of the "Received Items To Be Billed" report. `PurchaseInvoice.set_percentage_received` handles the received percentage on the invoice. If the invoice rate differs from the receipt rate, the difference is posted through `make_amount_difference_entry` in the receipt composer. It uses the receipt row's `amount_difference_with_purchase_invoice` field and the SRBNB account, so SRBNB still ends at zero.

final answer
ERPNext parks the value in the **Stock Received But Not Billed (SRBNB)** account, a liability-type clearing account. For fixed assets it uses **Asset Received But Not Billed**. Each company sets this account in the Company doctype field `stock_received_but_not_billed`. I read the posting code in the two `gl_composer.py` files and `expense_account.py`. I didn't run any of it. **Receipt side: `erpnext/stock/doctype/purchase_receipt/services/gl_composer.py`** - For each stock item, the receipt picks the clearing account. It is `asset_received_but_not_billed` if the row is a fixed asset, otherwise `stock_received_but_not_billed`. Both come from `doc.get_company_default(...)`. - For stock items (when perpetual inventory is on), it posts the entries in this order: 1. `make_item_asset_inward_gl_entry(d, stock_value_diff, stock_asset_account_name)` debits the warehouse's inventory account. The amount is the stock value difference from the Stock Ledger, found with `get_stock_value_difference`. 2. `make_stock_received_but_not_billed_entry(d)` posts the other leg. It books the item's `base_net_amount` as a negative debit (a credit) to SRBNB. For internal transfers it credits the from-warehouse account instead. 3. The same loop then adds the landed cost, expenses-included-in-valuation, amount-difference, sub-contracting and divisional-loss entries. - The result is Dr Stock In Hand and Cr SRBNB. The goods are on the books and the unpaid obligation sits in SRBNB until the bill arrives. **Billing side: Purchase Invoice** - `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/expense_account.py`, `ExpenseAccountService.set_expense_account`, is the first step. - For a stock item on a non-opening invoice, it forces the row's `expense_account` to SRBNB. The invoice does this when `update_stock` is off and the row isn't a drop-ship item. - If the row links to a Purchase Receipt, it first checks that the receipt actually posted a GL Entry to SRBNB. - If the invoice comes before any receipt, it still uses SRBNB. The receipt will later credit it, so the two net out. - Fixed-asset rows use `asset_received_but_not_billed` if the receipt booked it. Otherwise they use the CWIP or fixed-asset account. - `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py` posts the invoice entries: - The item loop debits each row's `expense_account`, which is SRBNB, for the billed amount. It credits the supplier's payable account. - The Dr on SRBNB cancels the Cr the receipt made, so the holding balance is cleared. - Any difference between the receipt and the bill goes elsewhere: - An exchange-rate difference between receipt and bill posts to the exchange gain/loss account (`exchange_gain_loss_account`). - Item tax that was valuation-capitalised but not booked in the receipt posts an extra Dr SRBNB (`negative_expense_to_be_booked`). - If the receipt used a provisional expense account, `get_provisional_accounts` and `make_provisional_gl_entry` reverse that provisional entry. The net effect is that SRBNB goes up (credit) on receipt and back down (debit) on billing, and the supplier payable replaces it. Any balance left in SRBNB is goods received but not yet billed. The `received_items_to_be_billed` report tracks that balance.

final answer
ERPNext parks the value in the **Stock Received But Not Billed (SRBNB)** account. It's a liability account, set as a company default. I read the code for this in `gl_composer.py`, `expense_account.py` and `purchase_receipt.py`. I did not run any of it or trace the exchange-rate and Purchase Price Variance branches in full. ## Receipt side (Purchase Receipt) - **Controller:** `PurchaseReceipt` in `erpnext/stock/doctype/purchase_receipt/purchase_receipt.py` extends `BuyingController`, which extends `StockController`. - **Posting:** `PurchaseReceipt.get_gl_entries()` (line 478) hands off to the service in `erpnext/stock/doctype/purchase_receipt/services/gl_composer.py`. - **Per-item entries:** `_make_item_gl_entries()` builds the entries for each item. - **Inventory side:** for a stock item it debits the warehouse's Stock In Hand account for the stock value difference. Fixed-asset items go to the asset account through `make_item_asset_inward_gl_entry`. - **Holding side:** the inner function `make_stock_received_but_not_billed_entry(item)` posts the opposite leg. The account is `stock_asset_rbnb`, which is the company's `stock_received_but_not_billed` default. For asset items it is `asset_received_but_not_billed`. - **Sign convention:** the amount is written as a debit of `-1 * outgoing_amount`, meaning a credit of the item's `base_net_amount`. If no account is set, `validate_account("Stock or Asset Received But Not Billed")` raises an error. So the receipt books **Dr Stock In Hand / Cr Stock Received But Not Billed**. There is no supplier payable yet. Other receipt-side code: - Landed cost and subcontracting entries come from `make_landed_cost_gl_entries` and `make_sub_contracting_gl_entries`. - `ProvisionalAccountingService.add_provisional_gl_entry` (`services/provisional_accounting.py`) is a separate provisional-expense path for non-stock items. - `PurchaseReceipt.update_billing_status()` (line 526) updates how much of the receipt has been billed. ## Billing side (Purchase Invoice) - **Choosing the account:** `ExpenseAccountService.set_expense_account()` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/expense_account.py` runs when perpetual inventory is on. For a stock item on an invoice that doesn't update stock, the item's `expense_account` is forced to SRBNB. - **Receipt check:** if the item has a `purchase_receipt`, it looks for a Purchase Receipt GL Entry on SRBNB. If one exists, it sets the account to SRBNB and warns "Expense Head changed" during validate. If there is no receipt, it also books to SRBNB. - **With Update Stock:** if the invoice itself updates stock, the expense account becomes the warehouse's inventory account instead. - **Posting:** `PurchaseInvoice.get_gl_entries()` (line 703) uses `services/gl_composer.py`. `make_item_gl_entries()` (line 129) creates, for each item, a **debit on `item.expense_account`** for the billed `base_amount`. That account is SRBNB, so it clears the credit the receipt left there. A code comment at about line 332 says this: when Update Stock is off, the invoice "only clears Stock Received But Not Billed at the full billed amount against the supplier". - **Supplier leg:** the credit goes to the supplier's payable account. - **Net effect:** **Dr SRBNB / Cr Creditors**. SRBNB returns to zero and the payable now exists. Adjustments around the clearing entry: - If the exchange rate differs from the receipt's, the code (around lines 353–370) adds a separate difference entry. - `make_stock_adjustment_entry` (line 535) books any difference between the billed amount and the amount the receipt booked. - With `update_stock` on, the invoice posts to inventory directly and bypasses SRBNB. Asset items use `asset_received_but_not_billed` (also set in `expense_account.py`). Cancelling the invoice reverses these entries. `BillingStatusService.cancel_provisional_entries()` is called from `purchase_invoice.py` around line 687.
Q16Group D — Numeric/logic edge casesIf a Pricing Rule's "Apply On" is set to "Item Code" and a user adds both an item template (a templated/variant-attribute item) an…
If a Pricing Rule's "Apply On" is set to "Item Code" and a user adds both an item template (a templated/variant-attribute item) and one of that template's own variants to the same rule's item list, what validation (if any) runs at save time, and what specifically does it check to detect and reject the conflicting configuration?
