final answer
Yes, a validation runs at save time. It lives in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/pricing_rule/pricing_rule.py`, on the `PricingRule` class. **Call path** - `PricingRule.validate()` calls `validate_duplicate_apply_on()`. - That method returns early if `apply_on` is "Transaction", or if `apply_on_dict` has no child table for the chosen value. - It takes the field name with `frappe.scrub(self.apply_on)`, which gives `item_code` here. It then collects every non-empty `item_code` from the rule's item child table. - If the list has repeats (`len(values) != len(set(values))`), it throws "Duplicate Item Code found in the table". - Only when `apply_on == "Item Code"` does it call `validate_template_with_variant(values)`. **What `validate_template_with_variant(item_codes)` checks** - It runs one `frappe.get_all("Item", ...)` query. The filters are `name IN item_codes` and `variant_of IN item_codes`, and it returns `name` and `variant_of`. - An item matches only if it is itself in the rule's list and its `variant_of` template is also in the list. That is exactly the template-plus-own-variant case. - If any rows come back, it takes the first one. It then calls `frappe.throw` with "Variant {0} and its template {1} cannot both be added to the same Pricing Rule", and the save is rejected. **Limits** - It only reports the first conflicting variant. - It only applies to the "Item Code" apply-on type, not to "Item Group" or "Brand". - A variant listed alongside a different template, one that isn't in the list, does not trigger it.

final answer
When a Pricing Rule is saved, `PricingRule.validate()` calls `validate_duplicate_apply_on()`. That method calls `validate_template_with_variant()` when the rule's "Apply On" is "Item Code". Both methods are in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/pricing_rule/pricing_rule.py`. I read them from the search output and did not open the full file, so I did not check the rest of `validate()`. **Step 1: `validate_duplicate_apply_on()`** - It does nothing when `apply_on` is "Transaction". - Otherwise it takes the child table for the chosen "Apply On" value (for "Item Code", the item codes table) and collects each row's `item_code`. - If the list contains repeats (`len(values) != len(set(values))`), it throws "Duplicate {0} found in the table". - If `apply_on == "Item Code"`, it then calls `validate_template_with_variant(values)`. **Step 2: `validate_template_with_variant(item_codes)`** - It runs one `frappe.get_all("Item", ...)` query with the filters `name in item_codes` and `variant_of in item_codes`. - That query returns an Item only when it is itself in the rule's list and its `variant_of` template is also in the list. In other words, it finds a variant whose template is in the same rule. - If it returns anything, the code takes the first result and throws: "Variant {0} and its template {1} cannot both be added to the same Pricing Rule". The message names the variant (`name`) and the template (`variant_of`). - The save is blocked. **Limits of the check** - It looks only at the Item master's `variant_of` field. It does not inspect `has_variants` or attributes. - It applies only to "Item Code". Item Group and Brand rules don't get it. - It reports only the first conflicting pair. **Related runtime behavior** - When rules are matched on a transaction, `_get_pricing_rules` in `utils.py` also matches rules set on an item's template. It does this with `item_code = variant_of`. - That is why a template and its variant in one rule would otherwise overlap.

On save, `PricingRule.validate()` (in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/pricing_rule/pricing_rule.py`) calls `validate_duplicate_apply_on()`. That method calls `validate_template_with_variant()` and rejects the rule. **`validate_duplicate_apply_on()`** - It runs only when `apply_on` is not "Transaction" and `apply_on_dict` has a child table for that value. - It builds `values` from the non-empty `item_code` field of each row in the `items` table. - If `len(values) != len(set(values))`, it throws "Duplicate Item Code found in the table". This catches exact repeats only, not a template and its variant. - When `apply_on == "Item Code"`, it then calls `self.validate_template_with_variant(values)`. **`validate_template_with_variant(item_codes)`** - It runs one `frappe.get_all("Item", ...)` query with two filters: `name IN item_codes` and `variant_of IN item_codes`. The fields returned are `name` and `variant_of`. - An Item row matches only if it is itself in the rule's list and its `variant_of` template is also in the list. That is exactly a variant listed together with its own template. - If any row matches, it throws: "Variant {0} and its template {1} cannot both be added to the same Pricing Rule". It takes the first match, `variants[0]`, and shows both names in bold. Save is blocked. The check is purely on the Item master's `variant_of` link and the item codes in the table. It does not look at `has_variants` or attributes. It applies only to the "Item Code" apply-on, so Item Group and Brand rules don't get it.

final answer
Yes, a check runs at save time and it rejects the combination. **Where it runs.** `PricingRule.validate()` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/pricing_rule/pricing_rule.py` calls `validate_duplicate_apply_on()`. That method does nothing if `apply_on` is "Transaction", or if `apply_on` has no entry in `apply_on_dict` (the lookup from `apply_on` to its child table). **Step by step:** 1. It finds the child table for the chosen "Apply On" and the matching field name, using `frappe.scrub(self.apply_on)`. For "Item Code" that field is `item_code`. 2. It collects the non-empty `item_code` values from the rows. 3. If the list contains repeats (`len(values) != len(set(values))`), it throws "Duplicate Item Code found in the table". 4. If `apply_on == "Item Code"`, it then calls `validate_template_with_variant(values)`. **The template/variant check.** `validate_template_with_variant(item_codes)` runs one database query: ```python frappe.get_all("Item", filters={"name": ("in", item_codes), "variant_of": ("in", item_codes)}, fields=["name", "variant_of"]) ``` It returns every Item that meets both conditions: - the item's own name is in the rule's item list, and - its `variant_of` field (the name of its template) is also in the rule's item list. If any such Item exists, it takes the first one and calls `frappe.throw`. The message is "Variant {0} and its template {1} cannot both be added to the same Pricing Rule", with the variant's name and its template's name in bold. The save is blocked. **Limits of the check.** - It runs only for "Item Code". Other Apply On values such as Item Group and Brand don't get it. - It compares only an item's direct `variant_of` against the list. It never reads the `has_variants` flag.
Q17Group D — Numeric/logic edge casesWhen two stock transactions share the exact same posting date and time (e.g. a Purchase Receipt and a Material Transfer both poste…
When two stock transactions share the exact same posting date and time (e.g. a Purchase Receipt and a Material Transfer both posted at the same second), how does ERPNext's stock ledger reposting logic determine the correct ordering/selection of "current" vs "previous" Stock Ledger Entries when one of those transactions is later cancelled, so that negative-stock validation isn't fooled by ambiguous same-timestamp ordering?

final answer
When two entries share the same `posting_datetime`, ERPNext breaks the tie with the Stock Ledger Entry's `creation` timestamp. It does not use the voucher number or the posting time alone. I read `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py` to answer this. I did not run anything, and I did not read the `update_entries_after` class body, so the mapping of these helpers onto the repost loop is partly inferred. **How ordering is defined** - Every Stock Ledger Entry stores a combined `posting_datetime` (posting date plus time). Ordering is always `posting_datetime, creation`. For "previous" lookups it is `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - `get_stock_ledger_entries()` is the generic fetcher. It always filters `is_cancelled = 0` and uses `order by posting_datetime {order}, creation {order}`. - `get_previous_sle()` calls `get_stock_ledger_entries()` with `<=`, `desc` and `limit 1`. When a reference SLE name is given, it adds `name != <sle>` so the entry can't match itself. **Picking the "previous" SLE of the current voucher** - `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher(args, operator="<", exclude_current_voucher=False)` selects with `is_cancelled = 0`, `order by posting_datetime desc, creation desc limit 1 for update`. - If `args` has `creation` and `sle_id` and the entry is not cancelled, it switches to `posting_datetime <= X and creation < <this creation>`. That means same-timestamp entries count as "previous" only if they were created earlier. - For a cancellation (`cancelled` set), the `creation` condition is skipped. - The `exclude_current_voucher` option adds `voucher_no != ...`. `get_stock_reco_qty_shift()` uses it for Stock Reconciliation. **Picking the "future" SLEs after a change** - `update_qty_in_future_sle()` builds `future_condition = posting_datetime > X`. For a non-cancelled entry it adds `OR (posting_datetime == X AND creation > this creation)`. - When the entry is cancelled (`is_cancelled` set), only `posting_datetime > X` is used, and the cancelled row itself is already excluded by `is_cancelled = 0`. - It then shifts `qty_after_transaction` on those rows by `actual_qty`. For a cancellation this shift is the reversal. - The update stops at the next Stock Reconciliation: - `get_next_stock_reco()` finds that reconciliation. - `get_next_reco_datetime_condition()` uses `>=` for a cancellation, otherwise `> OR (== AND creation >)`. - `get_datetime_limit_condition()` bounds the update with `< OR (== AND creation <)`. **Negative-stock validation** - `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle()` runs after the shift. - It skips the check if negative stock is allowed, or if `actual_qty >= 0` and the voucher is not a Stock Reconciliation. - It then calls `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty()`: - It looks for rows for the same item and warehouse with `voucher_no != current`, `posting_datetime >= X`, `is_cancelled = 0` and `qty_after_transaction < 0`. - It orders by `posting_datetime, creation` and takes the first one. - `>=` includes same-timestamp rows from other vouchers, and `creation` makes the order deterministic. - If the deficit is negative after `is_negative_with_precision()`, it throws `NegativeStockError` ("Insufficient Stock"). - For batches, `get_future_sle_with_negative_batch_qty()` computes a running `sum(actual_qty) over (order by posting_datetime, creation)`. **Related helpers** - `get_sle_by_voucher_detail_no()` returns `creation` along with the posting fields. - At roughly line 2584, a helper takes a `creation` argument and uses `posting_datetime < X OR (== X AND creation < creation)`. - Repost items carry `creation` through (lines 189, 342 and 751). Reposting therefore replays same-timestamp entries in their original creation order. So for a Purchase Receipt and a Material Transfer posted in the same second, the one created first is treated as earlier. When one is cancelled, its SLE is excluded by `is_cancelled = 0`. The remaining same-timestamp entries are ordered by `creation`, and the negative-stock check runs over that order.

final answer
ERPNext breaks same-timestamp ties with the Stock Ledger Entry's `creation` timestamp. The `cancelled` flag decides whether `creation` is used as a boundary or ignored. The code is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. **1. Ordering key.** Every comparison uses `posting_datetime` first and `creation` second. `posting_datetime` comes from `get_combine_datetime(posting_date, posting_time)` in `erpnext/stock/utils.py`. For example, `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` orders by `posting_datetime desc, creation desc` and takes `limit 1`. `get_next_stock_reco` and `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty` order by `posting_datetime` and then `creation`. **2. Reposting a voucher.** `repost_current_voucher` passes `creation`, `sle_id` and `cancelled` into `update_entries_after`. Two helpers then pick the entries: - `get_sle_against_current_voucher` selects non-cancelled SLEs for the item and warehouse with `posting_datetime == current`, ordered by `creation`. If the voucher is not being cancelled, it also requires `creation == kwargs.creation`, so it picks only that entry. If it is cancelled, that filter is dropped. All same-timestamp entries are then reprocessed in creation order through `process_sle_against_current_timestamp`. - `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` finds the "previous" SLE. If `creation`, `sle_id` and not `cancelled` are all set, it switches the operator to `<=` and adds `creation < '<creation>'`. So same-timestamp entries count as previous only if they were created earlier. For a cancelled voucher, no creation cut-off applies and the plain `<` on `posting_datetime` is used. The reposting then replays everything from that point, so nothing depends on the cancelled entry's position. `exclude_current_voucher=True` adds `voucher_no != current`, and the stock-reconciliation shift calculation uses it. **3. Shifting future balances.** `update_qty_in_future_sle` builds the "future" condition as `posting_datetime > current`. If the entry has `creation` and is not cancelled, it adds `OR (posting_datetime == current AND creation > current.creation)`. For a cancellation, which has `is_cancelled` set, only strictly later timestamps are shifted. The reversing quantity is applied to later entries, and same-second entries that are not strictly later are handled by the replay above. The shift stops at the next Stock Reconciliation, using `get_next_stock_reco`. That helper uses `get_next_reco_datetime_condition`, which is `>=` the current datetime on cancel and `>` or (`==` and `creation >`) otherwise. `get_datetime_limit_condition` bounds the range with `<` or (`==` and `creation <`). **4. Negative-stock validation.** `update_qty_in_future_sle` ends by calling `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle`. That calls `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty`, which looks for non-cancelled SLEs of other vouchers (`voucher_no !=`) with `posting_datetime >= current` and `qty_after_transaction < 0`. It sorts them by `posting_datetime` and `creation`, so the first offending entry is deterministic. A negative balance is reported as `NegativeStockError` and reported using the same ordering. The batch version, `get_future_sle_with_negative_batch_qty`, is separate; I didn't read its SQL. In short, a cancelled entry can't skew the validation. The cancel path drops the `creation` filters and uses full-timestamp ranges, and the replay recomputes balances in `(posting_datetime, creation)` order.

final answer
ERPNext breaks same-timestamp ties with the Stock Ledger Entry's `creation` timestamp, the row's insertion time. It uses `creation` as a secondary sort key everywhere in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. I read the code but did not run any scenario, so none of this is tested. **1. Ordering key** - `posting_datetime` is a combined date and time column. Every ledger query orders by `posting_datetime`, then `creation`. - `get_stock_ledger_entries()` (around line 1994) uses `order by posting_datetime {order}, creation {order}` and filters `is_cancelled = 0`. It is the base for `get_previous_sle()` (operator `<=`, desc, limit 1) and for `get_sle_after_datetime()` (operator `>`, asc, for update). - When a `name` is passed with `>` or `<=`, it adds `name != <sle>`. That excludes the reference entry itself. **2. Selecting the "previous" entry** - `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher(args, operator="<", exclude_current_voucher=False)` (line 1929) orders by `posting_datetime desc, creation desc` with `limit 1`, and only looks at non-cancelled rows. - If the call carries `creation` and `sle_id` and is not a cancellation, it switches to `<=` and adds `creation < <this sle's creation>`. Rows at the same timestamp that were inserted earlier then count as "previous", and later ones don't. - For a cancellation (`cancelled` is true), that creation filter is skipped. The entry being cancelled already has `is_cancelled = 1` and is excluded anyway. The previous entry is then simply the last live entry before the timestamp, whatever the creation order. - When a tie is not meant to be resolved by creation, `exclude_current_voucher=True` uses `voucher_no != current` instead. For example, line 2286 calls it with `<=` to read the last balance. **3. Selecting the "current" entries at the same timestamp** - `get_sle_against_current_voucher(kwargs)` (line 1906) returns all live SLEs for the item and warehouse where `posting_datetime ==` the current one. It sorts them by `creation asc`. - For a normal save it also requires `creation == kwargs.creation`, which pins the exact row. For a cancellation it drops that filter, so every same-timestamp entry is returned. In `process_sle_against_current_timestamp()` (line 938) each of those is re-processed in creation order. - The repost then reruns through `get_future_entries_to_fix()` and `get_sle_after_datetime()`, which are all ordered by `posting_datetime, creation`. The Purchase Receipt and the Material Transfer therefore replay in their original insertion order, and the running `qty_after_transaction` is rebuilt consistently. **4. Updating later entries** - `update_qty_in_future_sle()` (line 2212) treats an entry as "future" if `posting_datetime > current`. For a non-cancelled entry with a `creation` value, it also counts `posting_datetime == current AND creation > current.creation`. - On cancellation (`is_cancelled` set), only strictly later timestamps match, because the cancelled row no longer participates. That avoids shifting same-timestamp rows based on a row that no longer counts. - A similar `creation` comparison appears at line 2305 for entries after the current one, at line 2351 for "before the current detail", and in `get_stock_reco...` (line 2606: `posting_datetime <` or `(== AND creation <)`). **5. Negative-stock validation** - `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle()` (line 2355) calls `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty()` (line 2419). That query selects live SLEs with `voucher_no != this voucher`, `posting_datetime >= this posting_datetime`, and `qty_after_transaction < 0`. It orders by `posting_datetime, creation` and takes the first. - Only the first offending row is reported, and `creation` makes the choice deterministic when timestamps tie. - `is_negative_with_precision()` then ignores tiny rounding deficits. - The batch version, `get_future_sle_with_negative_batch_qty()`, uses a window function: `sum(actual_qty) over (order by posting_datetime, creation)`. - A cancelled voucher's rows have `is_cancelled = 1`, so they drop out of all of these queries. The remaining same-second rows are ordered by `creation`, which is why the balances can't be misread.

final answer
When two entries share the same `posting_datetime`, ERPNext breaks the tie with the Stock Ledger Entry's `creation` timestamp. For the cancel case, it stops using `creation` as a cut-off and uses the whole same-timestamp bucket instead. All of this is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. I did not read `process_sle_against_current_timestamp` or `build` themselves, so the exact cancel-path call sequence is partly inferred. **1. Sort key** - Every ordering query sorts by `posting_datetime` and then `creation`. SLEs carry a combined `posting_datetime` field built from posting date and time, and `creation` is the row's insert timestamp. - `get_stock_ledger_entries` ends with `order by posting_datetime {order}, creation {order}`. It is the shared helper behind `get_previous_sle` (`<=`, desc, limit 1), `get_sle_before_datetime` (`<`), `get_sle_after_datetime` and `get_future_entries_to_repost` (`>` and `>=`, asc, `for update`). - It filters on `is_cancelled = 0`, so cancelled rows never count as "previous" or "future". - With an `sle` name in the args, `get_previous_sle` also adds `name != sle`, so an entry never counts as its own predecessor. **2. Selecting "current" and "previous" in the reposting engine** - `repost_future_sle` passes `posting_date`, `posting_time` and `creation` of the affected SLE into `update_entries_after`. The `creation` value is the minimum for the item and warehouse, taken from `get_items_to_be_repost` (~line 498). - `get_sle_against_current_voucher` fetches the entries at the same item, warehouse and exact `posting_datetime`, with `is_cancelled = 0`, ordered by `creation asc` and locked `for update`. - On submit it adds `creation == kwargs.creation`, so only that one entry is picked from the same-second group. - On cancel (`kwargs.cancelled`) it drops the `creation` filter. It then takes all entries in that timestamp bucket in creation order, so the survivors, such as the Purchase Receipt and Material Transfer, are re-processed in order. - `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` supplies the opening balance via `initialize_previous_data`. - If `creation` and `sle_id` are given and the entry is not cancelled, it switches to `<=` with `creation < '<creation>'`. The previous entry is then only one that sorts strictly before the current one inside the same second. - Otherwise it uses `<` on `posting_datetime`, which means the previous entry comes from the earlier bucket. - With `exclude_current_voucher=True` (used by Stock Reconciliation) it excludes by `voucher_no` instead. - It orders `posting_datetime desc, creation desc limit 1`. **3. Pushing quantities forward and the negative-stock check** - `update_qty_in_future_sle` shifts `qty_after_transaction` on later rows. - Its future condition is `posting_datetime > X`. When the entry is not cancelled and has a `creation`, it adds `OR (posting_datetime == X AND creation > args.creation)`. - On cancellation (`is_cancelled`), the same-second `creation` branch is skipped. Only strictly later datetimes are shifted. - `get_next_stock_reco` and `get_next_reco_datetime_condition` use the same rule, so the shift stops at the next Stock Reconciliation. On cancel the condition is `posting_datetime >= current`. Otherwise it is `>` or (`==` and `creation >`). `get_datetime_limit_condition` bounds the range with `creation < detail.creation`. - `update_qty_in_future_sle` then calls `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle`. That calls `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty`, which looks for the first non-cancelled row with `qty_after_transaction < 0`, with the same voucher excluded. - It requires `posting_datetime >= sle_args["posting_datetime"]` and orders by `posting_datetime, creation`, with limit 1. - A row at the same second therefore counts. - The batch variant, `get_future_sle_with_negative_batch_qty`, uses a running sum `over (order by posting_datetime, creation)`. - The result is checked with `is_negative_with_precision` and raises `NegativeStockError`. The check is skipped if `allow_negative_stock` is set. It is also skipped for inward entries, unless the voucher is a Stock Reconciliation. - `validate_previous_sle_qty` only shows a msgprint warning. It does not block the posting. **Why this avoids the ambiguity:** same-second entries are ordered deterministically by `creation`, and the current entry is excluded by `name` or `voucher_no`. A cancelled entry is dropped from every query through `is_cancelled = 0`. After a cancel, all remaining same-timestamp entries are replayed in creation order, and the negative check runs over `>=` the timestamp. Cancelling the Purchase Receipt therefore exposes the Material Transfer's now-negative balance instead of hiding it.
Q18Group D — Numeric/logic edge casesWhen a Purchase Receipt is created for an internal (intra-company) stock transfer, how does the system determine the item's incomi…
When a Purchase Receipt is created for an internal (intra-company) stock transfer, how does the system determine the item's incoming rate, and does it use a value that was fixed at the time the source Delivery Note was made or one computed fresh at receipt time?

final answer
For an internal (intra-company) transfer, the Purchase Receipt uses the incoming rate that was fixed on the source Delivery Note Item. It does not recompute the rate from the receiving warehouse at receipt time. The one thing added at receipt time is any landed cost. **Where it happens:** `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`, in the stock-ledger processing method around lines 1032–1044, which is where each Stock Ledger Entry (SLE) is processed. 1. **Detecting the transfer.** The code runs when `sle.voucher_type` is "Purchase Receipt" or "Purchase Invoice", `sle.voucher_detail_no` is set, and `is_internal_transfer(sle)` is true. That function (line 2571) returns true when the document's `is_internal_supplier` is set and its `represents_company` equals its `company`. 2. **Looking up the rate.** The code calls `get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer(sle)` (line 2540). That function does the following: - It reads the `delivery_note_item` link from the Purchase Receipt Item. For a Purchase Invoice it reads `sales_invoice_item` and uses Sales Invoice Item instead. - It then reads the stored `incoming_rate` field of that Delivery Note Item. The docstring says this is the average of the outgoing rate. - It adds a landed-cost share: `landed_cost_voucher_amount / abs(sle.actual_qty)` from the Purchase Receipt Item. The return value is `rate + lcv_rate`. 3. **Applying the rate.** If the SLE is an outgoing leg (`actual_qty < 0`), `sle.outgoing_rate` is set to that rate. Otherwise, if the rate is non-zero, `sle.incoming_rate` is set to it. This overrides whatever `get_dynamic_incoming_outgoing_rate` would otherwise produce. The code comment says this stops a stale Purchase Receipt valuation rate from leaking the difference into COGS. I read only `stock_ledger.py`. I did not trace how the Delivery Note Item's `incoming_rate` is first computed when the Delivery Note is submitted. **Answer:** the rate is anchored to the value stored on the Delivery Note Item when the Delivery Note was made. It is not computed fresh at receipt time. Only the landed-cost adjustment is added at receipt time.

final answer
The incoming rate is the one fixed on the Delivery Note. It is read from the saved Delivery Note Item and not recalculated at receipt time. I traced this through the code and one test. I did not run anything. **1. On the Purchase Receipt (`BuyingController.set_incoming_rate`, `erpnext/controllers/buying_controller.py`)** - It only applies to a Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice that is an internal transfer and is not a return. - It calls `set_sales_incoming_rate_for_internal_transfer()`, which fills each item's `sales_incoming_rate`. - If the item is linked to a Delivery Note Item (`delivery_note_item`), the value comes from that row: - When the supplier is an internal supplier (`is_internal_supplier`), it reads the Delivery Note Item's stored `incoming_rate`. - Otherwise it reads the Delivery Note Item's `rate`. - Either way the value is multiplied by the item's `conversion_factor`. - Only if the item has no Delivery Note link does it compute a rate fresh. In that case it calls `get_incoming_rate` for the `from_warehouse` at the receipt's posting date and time. - Unless Stock Settings has `allow_internal_transfer_at_arms_length_price` on, the item `rate` is then overwritten with `sales_incoming_rate`. The discount and margin fields are zeroed, and the user gets a message that the rate was updated to the valuation rate. **2. In the stock ledger (`get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer`, `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`)** - `update_entries_after.get_incoming_outgoing_rate_from_transaction` calls this function for a Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice SLE that is an internal transfer. - It looks up `delivery_note_item` (or `sales_invoice_item` for a Purchase Invoice). - It returns that Delivery Note Item's stored `incoming_rate`, plus the Purchase Receipt item's `landed_cost_voucher_amount` divided by the absolute SLE quantity. **Result:** the rate was set when the Delivery Note was submitted. That is the outgoing valuation rate from the source warehouse, saved as `incoming_rate` on the Delivery Note Item. The Purchase Receipt reuses it, and the stock ledger re-reads it from the Delivery Note Item whenever it reposts. The only addition at receipt time is landed cost voucher charges. The test `test_internal_transfer_pr_incoming_sle_anchored_to_dn_rate` in `test_purchase_receipt.py` supports this. It sets the Purchase Receipt item's `valuation_rate` to 80 and its `sales_incoming_rate` to 0, then reposts. The SLE goes back to the Delivery Note's 100.

final answer
**Short answer:** For a linked internal transfer, the rate is the value stored on the source Delivery Note Item. It was fixed when the Delivery Note was made. It isn't recomputed at receipt time. Recomputation happens only when the Purchase Receipt has no link to a Delivery Note Item. I traced the rate-setting code. I didn't read `stock_ledger.py` or run anything. **Where this happens:** `BuyingController.set_incoming_rate()` in `erpnext/controllers/buying_controller.py` (~L606). It returns early unless the document is a Purchase Receipt, or a Purchase Invoice with `update_stock`. It also returns early for returns and for non-internal transfers. Otherwise it calls `set_sales_incoming_rate_for_internal_transfer()` (~L647), which fills `d.sales_incoming_rate` on each item. **How `sales_incoming_rate` is chosen, per item:** - **Linked to a Delivery Note Item (`delivery_note_item` set):** the code reads a value from that Delivery Note Item with `frappe.db.get_value("Delivery Note Item", ..., field)` and multiplies it by `conversion_factor`. - If `is_internal_supplier` is set, the field is `incoming_rate`. This is the valuation or outgoing rate the Delivery Note stored when it posted stock out of the source warehouse. - Otherwise the field is `rate`. - For the Purchase Invoice, the same logic reads `Sales Invoice Item` through `sales_invoice_item`. - **Not linked:** this is a fallback. The code calls `erpnext.stock.utils.get_incoming_rate()` for `from_warehouse` at the Purchase Receipt's posting date and time, with a negative qty. The rate is then computed fresh from stock ledger valuation at receipt time. **How the rate is applied:** Back in `set_incoming_rate()`, the item's `rate` is overwritten with `sales_incoming_rate`. Discount, margin and discount amount are zeroed, and the user sees a message that the rate was updated. This overwrite is skipped if the Stock Settings option `allow_internal_transfer_at_arms_length_price` is enabled. In that case the user's rate stands. **Stock ledger side:** `update_stock_ledger` in the same file builds the inward Stock Ledger Entry with `incoming_rate = d.valuation_rate` and `recalculate_rate = 1` when `from_warehouse` is set. The valuation rate is derived from the overwritten item rate. A test in `test_purchase_receipt.py` (~L1913) is titled "Internal-transfer PR's inward SLE must use DN.incoming_rate even when…". It supports the view that the Delivery Note's stored `incoming_rate` is what drives the inward entry.

final answer
**Short answer:** the incoming rate comes from the source Delivery Note's own `incoming_rate`, which the system stored on the Delivery Note Item. It is not computed fresh from the receiving warehouse. The only part added at receipt time is any landed cost. **How the rate is determined** 1. In `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`, `update_entries_after.process_sle` runs when the Purchase Receipt's stock ledger entry (SLE) is processed. It first computes the normal dynamic rate. It then overrides that rate if the voucher is a Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice, has a `voucher_detail_no`, and `is_internal_transfer(sle)` is true. Around lines 1032–1044, it does this: - It calls `get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer(sle)`. - For an outgoing SLE (`actual_qty < 0`), it sets `sle.outgoing_rate` to that rate. - Otherwise, if the rate is non-zero, it sets `sle.incoming_rate` to it. - The code comment says this anchors both legs to the Delivery Note's rate. Otherwise a stale Purchase Receipt `valuation_rate` would leak the gap into COGS (cost of goods sold) as a divisional loss. 2. `is_internal_transfer(sle)` (same file, around line 2571) is true when the Purchase Receipt has `is_internal_supplier` set and `represents_company` equals `company`. 3. `get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer(sle)` (same file, line 2540) works in three steps: - It reads the Purchase Receipt Item's `delivery_note_item` link. For a Purchase Invoice it reads `sales_invoice_item` instead. - It fetches `incoming_rate` from that Delivery Note Item (or Sales Invoice Item) using `frappe.get_cached_value`. - It adds a landed cost term: `landed_cost_voucher_amount / abs(sle.actual_qty)` from the Purchase Receipt Item, if there is one. - It returns `rate + lcv_rate`. The docstring says "incoming rate is the average of the outgoing rate". **Fixed or fresh?** The base rate is a stored value, not a fresh calculation at receipt time. The Delivery Note Item's `incoming_rate` is written when the Delivery Note's own SLE is posted. I inferred this from the code around `stock_ledger.py` line 1563, which writes the outgoing rate back to the voucher item's `incoming_rate`. I did not read that code closely. The Purchase Receipt just looks the stored value up. That stored value is not strictly frozen, though. If the Delivery Note's valuation is reposted, its item `incoming_rate` changes, and a later Purchase Receipt (re)posting picks up the new figure. The landed cost addition is the one part that depends on the Purchase Receipt itself.
Q19Group D — Numeric/logic edge casesWhen a Purchase Invoice is created against a Standard Cost item's Purchase Receipt with "Update Stock" switched off, what GL entri…
When a Purchase Invoice is created against a Standard Cost item's Purchase Receipt with "Update Stock" switched off, what GL entries does the invoice post for the item's expense side, and how is that different from an invoice with Update Stock enabled?

final answer
**Short answer:** with Update Stock off, the invoice posts a single debit for the item, with no Purchase Price Variance (PPV) entry. With Update Stock on, it can post a second debit to the PPV account. The code is `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer.make_item_gl_entries` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`. I didn't trace where `item.expense_account` gets set, so the SRBNB point below rests on the code comment, not on that. **Update Stock off** - The invoice goes through the `else:` branch of `make_item_gl_entries`, because the `doc.update_stock and doc.auto_accounting_for_stock` check fails. - It posts one debit to `expense_account` for the full billed amount (`base_amount` from `tax_service.get_amount_and_base_amount`), against the supplier. - For an item linked to a Purchase Receipt, that account is "Stock Received But Not Billed" (SRBNB). The code comment says the invoice only clears SRBNB at the full billed amount. - The matching credit is the supplier payable, from `make_supplier_gl_entry`. - There is no inventory entry and no PPV entry. The Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at the standard cost and the PPV. The comment says booking PPV again would double count it and leave SRBNB partly uncleared. - If the exchange rate differs from the receipt, a pair of entries (expense account debit, exchange gain/loss credit) is added. **Update Stock on** - The invoice goes through the stock branch and calls `make_stock_adjustment_entry`. - The invoice's own Stock Ledger Entry sets the inventory value. `warehouse_debit_amount` is `valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor`. - The `expense_account` debit is that `warehouse_debit_amount`, not the billed amount. That amount is also posted as the inventory debit. - If the invoice amount differs from the stock value in the ledger, an extra "Stock Adjustment" debit is posted. This applies when `warehouse_debit_amount` differs from the Stock Ledger Entry's `stock_value_difference`, or on returns. - That extra entry goes to the account from `get_stock_variance_account`. For a Standard Cost item this is `get_purchase_price_variance_account(item_code, company)`, the PPV account. For other items it is the company's `default_expense_account`. - The amount is `warehouse_debit_amount − stock_value_difference`, or the reverse on a return without a return-against document. - `warehouse_debit_amount` is then reset to the stock value, so the expense or SRBNB debit matches the stock value. - Landed cost, subcontracting raw-material cost and `from_warehouse` entries can also be posted here.

final answer
**With Update Stock off**, the invoice posts only two lines for the item. It debits the item's `expense_account` for the full billed amount and credits the supplier. It posts no Purchase Price Variance (PPV) line. This comes from the `else` branch of `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer.make_item_gl_entries` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py` (lines 319–350). That branch runs because the stock-path condition `doc.update_stock and doc.auto_accounting_for_stock and item in stock_items` is false. - **Debit:** `expense_account` is `item.expense_account`, or `deferred_expense_account` if deferred expense is enabled and the invoice isn't a return. For a stock item linked to a Purchase Receipt, I expect this to be "Stock Received But Not Billed". I did not check where that account gets set. The debit is the full `base_net_amount`, taken from `tax_service.get_amount_and_base_amount`, with `against` set to the supplier. - **Credit:** the payable to the supplier, written by `make_supplier_gl_entry`. - **Why no PPV:** a code comment in that branch says the Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at standard cost and the PPV. If the invoice booked PPV again, it would double count and leave Stock Received But Not Billed partly uncleared. So `get_stock_variance_account` is never called on this path. - **Optional extra:** if the Purchase Receipt and the invoice used different exchange rates (and "set landed cost based on purchase invoice rate" is off), there is one more pair. It is a debit to the expense account and a credit to the exchange gain/loss account for the difference. **With Update Stock on**, the invoice receives the stock itself, so it takes the first branch: 1. `make_stock_adjustment_entry` works out `warehouse_debit_amount`. This is `valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor`, the amount booked at standard cost. 2. It compares that amount with the Stock Ledger Entry's `stock_value_difference`. If they differ, it posts a stock adjustment line to the account from `get_stock_variance_account(item)`. For a Standard Cost item that is the Purchase Price Variance account from `get_purchase_price_variance_account`. For other items it is the company's `default_expense_account`. This line is a debit of `warehouse_debit_amount − stock_amount`. 3. It then debits `item.expense_account` with `warehouse_debit_amount`, or with `base_net_amount` when `from_warehouse` is set. The `from_warehouse` case also moves value between the two inventory accounts. 4. It adds landed-cost credits and subcontracting raw-material credits when they apply. **Difference:** with Update Stock on, the invoice both books the stock and posts any PPV, which for a Standard Cost item is the gap between the billed or valuation amount and the standard value. With it off, the invoice only clears Stock Received But Not Billed against the supplier at the full billed amount, and the Purchase Receipt carries the standard-cost and PPV entries.

final answer
With Update Stock off, the invoice posts only one expense-side debit per item. That debit is to the item's `expense_account`, for the full billed amount, with the supplier payable credited in the supplier entry. It posts no Purchase Price Variance (PPV) entry and no inventory entry. With Update Stock on, the invoice also posts a stock-adjustment line to the PPV account for Standard Cost items. I did not open `ExpenseAccountService` (`purchase_invoice/services/expense_account.py`). So I'm inferring, not confirming, that the item's `expense_account` is Stock Received But Not Billed when a Purchase Receipt is linked. All of this is in `PurchaseInvoice`'s GL composer, `make_item_gl_entries()` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`. **Update Stock off** - The item takes the `else` branch (around lines 319–350), not the stock branch. - It posts a debit to `item.expense_account` (the deferred expense account if deferred expense is enabled) for `base_net_amount`, with `against = supplier`. - A code comment says the linked Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at standard cost and the PPV. The invoice therefore only clears Stock Received But Not Billed (SRBNB) at the full billed amount against the supplier. - The comment also says booking PPV again would double count it and leave SRBNB partly uncleared. Any gap between the billed price and the standard cost stays as PPV from the Purchase Receipt. - The only extra entries are an optional exchange-rate difference pair (a debit to the expense account and a credit to the exchange gain/loss account), provisional-accounting reversals, and the valuation-tax SRBNB debit for `item_tax_amount`. **Update Stock on** (and perpetual inventory is on) - The item takes the stock branch (lines 180–317). The invoice creates the stock ledger entries itself. - `make_stock_adjustment_entry()` compares the item's `valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor` with the stock ledger entry's `stock_value_difference`. - For a Standard Cost item, the stock ledger entry is valued at the standard rate. If the two amounts differ, it posts a debit "Stock Adjustment" line to the account from `get_stock_variance_account()`. - That function returns the Purchase Price Variance account from `get_purchase_price_variance_account()` in `item_standard_cost.py`. It uses the Item Default `purchase_price_variance_account` first, then the Company's `default_purchase_price_variance_account`. - Other items use the Company default expense account instead. - The expense-account debit (`item.expense_account`, normally the stock-in-hand or SRBNB account) is then for `warehouse_debit_amount`, which is the standard-cost stock value. It is not the full billed amount, so the PPV line takes up the difference. - The returns branch (`is_return` with Update Stock) does the same adjustment from `stock_amount − warehouse_debit_amount`. - Landed cost voucher credits and subcontracting raw-material credits are also posted in this branch.

final answer
**Update Stock off** The invoice posts only one expense-side entry per item. It's built in the `else` branch of `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer.make_item_gl_entries` (`erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`, about lines 319–350). - **Debit:** `item.expense_account`. For a stock item this is normally Stock Received But Not Billed. If deferred expense is enabled and the invoice isn't a return, it's `item.deferred_expense_account` instead. - **Amount:** the full billed net amount (`base_amount` from `tax_service.get_amount_and_base_amount`), with `against` set to the supplier. - **Credit:** the payable entry to the supplier is built separately, and it's the other side of this debit. The code comment says why there's nothing more. The Purchase Receipt has already booked the stock at the standard cost and the Purchase Price Variance (PPV). The invoice only clears Stock Received But Not Billed (SRBNB) at the billed amount. Booking PPV again would double count it and leave SRBNB partly uncleared. Two small extras can appear. Provisional-accounting reversals come from `make_provisional_gl_entry` if that company setting is on. A foreign-currency exchange-rate difference against the Purchase Receipt can post an expense debit and an exchange gain/loss credit. Neither relates to Standard Cost. **Update Stock on** This applies when `update_stock` and `auto_accounting_for_stock` are on and the item is a stock item or fixed asset. The invoice then creates the stock itself, so it books both sides. - **Valuation amount:** `make_stock_adjustment_entry` computes `warehouse_debit_amount` as `valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor`. - **Expense debit:** that valuation amount is debited to `item.expense_account`. The inventory (warehouse) account entries are added by the stock controller's `get_gl_entries`, not here. - **Variance debit:** if the valuation amount differs from the Stock Ledger Entry's `stock_value_difference`, an extra "Stock Adjustment" debit is posted. For a Standard Cost item the account comes from `get_stock_variance_account`, which returns the item's Purchase Price Variance account (`get_purchase_price_variance_account`). Other items use the company's default expense account. The expense debit is then reset to the stock value. - **Returns:** a return without `return_against` can also post a variance entry. - **Other entries:** landed-cost credits, subcontracting raw-material credits, and internal-transfer from-warehouse entries. **Difference** With Update Stock off, the invoice debits only SRBNB for the billed amount and never touches the PPV account. With Update Stock on, it debits the expense account at valuation and posts any difference from the standard-cost stock value to PPV. I read the code but didn't run it.

Several ERPNext queries pick a single "latest" row using ORDER BY some-date DESC with LIMIT 1. How does the codebase guarantee those picks are deterministic when several rows share the same date — what is the pattern, why does it matter across database engines, and where is it applied? Name concrete functions and files.

final answer
The codebase makes these picks deterministic by adding secondary sort keys after the date, so the ordering is total. I found no shared helper for this. Each call site spells out its own tie-breakers. I read the files below and searched the rest by pattern, so the list may not be exhaustive. **The pattern** The sort is `date DESC`, then `creation DESC`, then sometimes `name DESC`, with `LIMIT 1`. `creation` is the row's insert timestamp. `name` is the primary key, so it is always unique. - `date DESC, creation DESC` is enough in most cases, because `creation` is almost always distinct. - Adding `name DESC` gives a fully unique, total ordering. This matters when `creation` can also collide, for example in bulk or test inserts. - The same idea shows up in the query-builder form as chained `.orderby(col, order=frappe.qb.desc)` calls. An example is `.orderby(sle.posting_datetime, order=frappe.qb.desc).orderby(sle.creation, order=frappe.qb.desc)`. **Why it matters across engines** If rows tie on the date, SQL doesn't define which one `LIMIT 1` returns. MariaDB/MySQL and PostgreSQL then differ, because their plans and index scan order differ. Without tie-breakers, MariaDB and Postgres can return different rows, and results can vary between runs, which makes CI flaky. A comment in `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py` says this directly: "total order so the latest SLE is picked identically on both engines (was posting_date only)". Other code in the repo follows the same cross-engine reasoning: - `erpnext/selling/page/point_of_sale/point_of_sale.py` (around line 467) has a "tiebreaker" comment so the same contact is picked on MariaDB and Postgres. - `erpnext/selling/report/customer_acquisition_and_loyalty/customer_acquisition_and_loyalty.py` (around line 169) has a comment that a name tie-break makes the first-seen classification "deterministic across engines". - The `Max()`/`Min()` wrapping in `erpnext/manufacturing/doctype/production_plan/services/bom_explosion.py` is the same idea applied to GROUP BY. **Where it's applied** - `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py` - The valuation-rate lookup, which picks the latest Stock Ledger Entry, uses `posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc` (around line 322). This is the fullest form. - Another lookup near line 82 uses `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - `erpnext/manufacturing/doctype/bom/bom.py`: `_get_last_valuation_rate_from_sle` orders by `posting_datetime DESC, creation DESC` with `LIMIT 1`. - `erpnext/setup/utils.py`: the Currency Exchange lookup in `get_exchange_rate` uses `date desc, name desc`. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py` - `ItemStandardCost.get_last_standard_cost` uses `effective_date desc, creation desc`. - `get_item_standard_rate` uses the same ordering. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_closing_entry/stock_closing_entry.py` (around line 345): `to_date desc, creation desc`. - Tests that mirror the same ordering: `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_entry/test_stock_entry.py` and `erpnext/stock/report/stock_balance/test_stock_balance.py`, both with `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - `erpnext/accounts/report/gross_profit/gross_profit.py` chains `.orderby` calls, for example on `posting_date` and then further columns, for the same purpose.

final answer
**The pattern.** A date is not unique, so `ORDER BY date DESC LIMIT 1` leaves the winner undefined when rows tie on that date. ERPNext fixes this by appending tie-breaker columns to the sort until the ordering is total: `date desc, creation desc, name desc` - `creation` is the insertion timestamp. - `name` is the primary key, so it is unique and is the final guarantee. - Each site is usually marked by a comment such as "total order", "tie-break" or "tiebreaker". **Why it matters across engines.** SQL does not define the order of rows that tie on the sort key. MariaDB/MySQL and Postgres can return different rows, depending on the plan, index choice and physical layout. The result can even change between runs. A `LIMIT 1` over a non-total order could therefore give different incoming rates or valuation rates on each engine. A total order makes the same row win on both. A related Postgres rule shows up in some of these sites. When a query uses `SELECT DISTINCT`, every `ORDER BY` column must also be in the select list. That is why `creation` is added to the selected columns in two places: - `rfq_transaction_list` in `erpnext/controllers/website_list_for_contact.py` - the SLE query near line 3426 of `erpnext/stock/doctype/serial_and_batch_bundle/serial_and_batch_bundle.py` **Where it is applied.** Latest-by-date picks with `LIMIT 1`: - `update_entries_after.get_incoming_value_for_serial_nos` in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py` (around lines 1653–1656). It orders by `posting_date`, `creation` and `name`, all descending, with `limit(1)`, to get the latest incoming rate for a serial number moved to another company. `test_incoming_value_for_transferred_serial_no_is_deterministic` in `stock_ledger_entry/test_stock_ledger_entry.py` covers it. It inserts two same-date SLEs, `MAT-SLE-TIE-A` and `MAT-SLE-TIE-B`, and expects the later name to win. - `set_stock_balance_as_per_serial_no` in `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py` (around line 322). It uses `order_by="posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc"`, and a comment says it was previously `posting_date` only. - `get_item_standard_rate` in `erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py` (around line 306). It orders by `effective_date desc, creation desc`. Another query in the same file, around line 265, uses the same ordering. - Other `creation desc` tie-breakers on a date or datetime: `stock_balance.py` (around line 82, `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`) and `stock_closing_entry.py` (around line 345, `to_date desc, creation desc`). I did not find a tie-breaker on `RepostAccountingLedger.validate_for_closed_fiscal_year` in `repost_accounting_ledger.py`. It uses `order_by="period_end_date desc"` and `posting_date desc` with `limit=1`. That is harmless there, because it only reads the date value, so ties return the same date. The same idea applies to non-date `LIMIT 1` picks, with `name` as the final tie-break: - `get_payer_address_html` and `get_street_address_html` in `erpnext/regional/report/irs_1099/irs_1099.py` add `.orderby(address.name)` or `.orderby(link.parent)`. `test_irs_1099.py` covers them. - The price-list query in `erpnext/stock/get_item_details.py` (around line 1267) ends with `orderby(ip.name, desc)` as a "final unique tiebreaker". - `packed_item.py` uses `order_by="qty desc, name asc"`. - The first-seen-customer query in `customer_acquisition_and_loyalty.py` uses `order_by="posting_date, name"`.

final answer
**The pattern.** `ORDER BY <date> DESC` alone isn't a total order. If several rows share the same date, the row that `LIMIT 1` returns depends on the engine and the query plan. So ERPNext appends tie-breaker columns after the date. The breakers are `creation` (the insert timestamp) and, where even that can tie, `name` (the primary key, which is unique). That makes the ordering total, so the same row wins every time. A comment in `stock_balance.py` states the intent directly: "total order so the latest SLE is picked identically on both engines (was posting_date only)". **Why it matters across engines.** MariaDB/MySQL and PostgreSQL don't promise any particular order among rows that compare equal. Which row they return depends on the index used, the plan and the physical layout. With a date-only sort, the same data could give different "latest" rows on the two engines. It could also change between runs on a single engine. For stock valuation, exchange rates and standard cost, that would mean different valuation rates or exchange rates for the same data. Adding `creation` and `name` removes that ambiguity. **Where it is applied.** I found these with a grep for `order_by` clauses that sort by a date descending and then by `creation` or `name`. It may not be exhaustive. - `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py` - `repost_stock`, around line 322, is the loop that picks the latest Stock Ledger Entry (SLE) per item and warehouse. It uses `order_by="posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc"` with `limit=1`. This is the fullest version, with a `name` tie-breaker as well. - Line 82 uses `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py` - `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` (around line 1929) is a raw SQL query. It uses `order by posting_datetime desc, creation desc limit 1 for update`, so it picks the previous SLE for valuation deterministically and locks it. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py` - `ItemStandardCost.get_last_standard_cost` and the module-level `get_item_standard_rate` both use `effective_date desc, creation desc` with `limit=1`. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_closing_entry/stock_closing_entry.py` uses `to_date desc, creation desc` (line 345). - `erpnext/setup/utils.py` - The latest `Currency Exchange` row is picked with `order_by="date desc, name desc"` and `limit=1`. This is the lookup that returns `exchange_rate`. - Tests that mirror the same ordering: - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_entry/test_stock_entry.py` - `erpnext/stock/report/stock_balance/test_stock_balance.py` - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_reconciliation/test_stock_reconciliation.py`, which uses `posting_time desc, creation desc`. **Related, but not the same.** `erpnext/accounts/doctype/bank_clearance/bank_clearance.py` chains `.orderby(posting_date).orderby(name, order=Order.desc)` in its query-builder queries. `payment_entry.py` does `.orderby(transaction_date).orderby(name)`. Both use a unique `name` as a secondary sort for stable listings. Neither is a `LIMIT 1` pick.

final answer
**The pattern.** ERPNext never relies on the date column alone to pick the "latest" row. It adds tie-breakers so the sort is a total order, then applies `LIMIT 1`. The usual form is `ORDER BY <date> DESC, creation DESC`. Where `creation` can also tie, it adds a final unique key: `ORDER BY posting_date DESC, creation DESC, name DESC`. **Why it matters across engines.** If several rows share the same date, SQL doesn't define which one comes first. MariaDB/MySQL and PostgreSQL each choose differently, depending on the plan, the index used and physical row order. The same query can then return different rows on different engines, or on different runs. `creation` is a timestamp with microseconds, so it usually separates same-day rows. `name` is the primary key, so it is always unique and settles any remaining tie. The code states this intent in one comment. In `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py`, inside `repost_stock_for_item_warehouse` (the SLE lookup at about line 317), it reads: "total order so the latest SLE is picked identically on both engines (was posting_date only)". That shows the pattern was added to fix an ambiguous `posting_date`-only sort. **Where it is applied.** These are the sites I found. The grep was not exhaustive, so there may be more. - `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py` - `get_balance_qty_from_sle` sorts by `posting_datetime desc, creation desc` with `limit=1`, to get the latest `qty_after_transaction`. - The SLE lookup at about line 322 sorts by `posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc` with `limit=1`. This is the full three-key form. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py` - `ItemStandardCost.get_last_standard_cost` sorts by `effective_date desc, creation desc`, `limit=1`. - `get_item_standard_rate` uses the same sort to find the latest submitted cost whose effective date is on or before the posting date. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_closing_entry/stock_closing_entry.py` (about line 345) sorts by `to_date desc, creation desc`. - Query-builder variants use `.orderby(..., creation, order=Order.desc)` as a tie-breaker. They appear in: - `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py` - `erpnext/stock/deprecated_serial_batch.py` - `erpnext/stock/doctype/bin/bin.py` - `erpnext/stock/doctype/serial_and_batch_bundle/serial_and_batch_bundle.py` - `erpnext/assets/doctype/asset/depreciation.py` - `erpnext/accounts/report/gross_profit/gross_profit.py`, which orders by warehouse, `posting_datetime` and `creation` - Tests use the same ordering when they fetch the latest SLE, so they match the production picks. Examples are `test_stock_entry.py`, `stock_balance/test_stock_balance.py` and `test_stock_reconciliation.py`.

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext uses Postgres advisory locks keyed on (item, warehouse). MariaDB gets the same serialization from gap locks and `FOR UPDATE` instead. I read the code and its comments but didn't run any of it. **Why Postgres needs its own locks.** MariaDB's gap locks and `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` make concurrent writers take turns. Postgres has no gap locks, and its locking reads can't see rows another transaction is inserting. Without extra locking, two writers could both compute from the same stale previous Stock Ledger Entry (SLE) and the same batch history. That would double-compute the average rate and lose a Bin write. **Three layers of locking in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py` and `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py`:** 1. **`repost_gate(item_code, warehouse)`** (`stock_ledger.py`, around line 276): - It returns `frappe.db.advisory_lock(("stock_repost", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. The timeout is 300 seconds. On MariaDB it returns `nullcontext()`. - `repost_future_sle` wraps each item-warehouse repost in `with repost_gate(...)` (around line 335). - This stops two background Repost Item Valuation jobs from reposting the same pair at once, which avoids deadlock and retry churn. - It deliberately covers repost against repost only. The synchronous `repost_current_voucher` submit path isn't gated, so a submit never blocks behind a background repost. 2. **`sle_processing_gate(item_code, warehouse)`** (`stock_ledger.py`, around line 292): - It calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("stock-sle", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. This lock is held until commit or rollback and can be taken again by the same transaction. - It is called when SLEs are written, around line 120 in the entry-writing code. That code loops over `sorted({(item_code, warehouse)})`, so two vouchers touching the same pairs always take locks in the same order and can't deadlock each other. - It is also called in the SLE processing class around line 613. 3. **`BatchNoValuation.calculate_avg_rate`** (`serial_batch_bundle.py`, lines 818–845): - For outward entries (`actual_qty <= 0`) on Postgres, it first calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("batch-valuation", item_code, warehouse))`. Only then does it read the batch history in `get_batch_stock_before_date`. - This makes the batch average-rate computation single-writer per item-warehouse. Row-locking the whole history would write a lock marker on every tuple, which the advisory lock avoids. - In `deprecated_serial_batch.py` (around lines 147–160 and 268–292), the grouped `.for_update()` is skipped on Postgres. It is applied only when `db_type != "postgres"`, because the caller already holds the advisory lock. All the lock keys are tuples, so an item code or warehouse name containing a colon can't map two different pairs to the same lock. The `hasattr(frappe.db, "advisory_lock")` checks mean an older Frappe falls back to no gate instead of failing the job. **Recovery if a deadlock still happens.** In `repost_item_valuation.py`, `RecoverableErrors = (JobTimeoutException, QueryDeadlockError, QueryTimeoutError)`. The error handler around line 580 rolls back and sets the status to "In Progress" for these errors, so the job is retried. Anything else is marked "Failed". Classifying by exception type matters because the old string match only recognised MariaDB's "Deadlock found" and missed Postgres's "deadlock detected".

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext uses three layers of advisory locks keyed on (item, warehouse). Row locks and deadlock retry back them up. I read the code but did not run it. **1. Repost-vs-repost gate: `repost_gate()` in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`** - `repost_future_sle()` wraps its `update_entries_after(...)` call in `with repost_gate(item_code, warehouse):`. - On Postgres the gate takes a session-level `frappe.db.advisory_lock(("stock_repost", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. The timeout is 300s. - Two background reposts of the same pair therefore take turns. They no longer race into a lock-order deadlock on the inner `... for update` row locks. - The key is a tuple, so a colon in an item or warehouse name can't make two different pairs share a lock. - If the wait times out, a recoverable `QueryTimeoutError` re-queues the job and frees the worker slot. - MariaDB gets `nullcontext()` and uses only the plain deadlock-retry path. - If `frappe.db` has no `advisory_lock` (an older frappe), the gate quietly does nothing. - The synchronous submit-time `repost_current_voucher` path is deliberately not gated. Blocking a submit behind a background repost was judged worse than retrying. **2. Stock-write gate: `sle_processing_gate()` in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`** - It takes a transaction-scoped `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("stock-sle", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. The lock is re-entrant and released at commit or rollback. - It is called from `make_sl_entries()` (around line 120) and from `update_entries_after` (around line 613). - `make_sl_entries()` locks the pairs in sorted order, so two vouchers touching the same pairs can't take the locks in opposite order. That removes a deadlock source. - This stands in for MariaDB's gap locks. Postgres locking reads can't see rows another transaction is inserting. Without the gate, two writers would compute from the same stale previous Stock Ledger Entry, and one Bin write would be lost. **3. Batch average-rate gate: `BatchNoValuation.calculate_avg_rate()` in `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py`** - For outward entries on Postgres it first calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("batch-valuation", sle.item_code, sle.warehouse))`. - Only then does it run `get_batch_stock_before_date()`, which sums `stock_value_difference` and `qty` per batch to get the average rate. - Only one transaction at a time computes the average for an item-warehouse, so the same average isn't computed twice from stale history. - On MariaDB that grouped query uses `.for_update()`, which takes gap locks. On Postgres the code skips `for_update`, because `FOR UPDATE` is invalid with `GROUP BY`. Row-locking the whole history would also write a lock marker on every tuple. **Fallback if a deadlock still happens: `repost()` in `repost_item_valuation.py`** - `RecoverableErrors = (JobTimeoutException, QueryDeadlockError, QueryTimeoutError)`. - The check is by exception type, not by a traceback string match. The old string match only recognised MariaDB's "Deadlock found" and missed Postgres's "deadlock detected". - A recoverable error rolls back the transaction and sets the Repost Item Valuation to `In Progress`, so it is retried. Any other error sets it to `Failed`.

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext uses two advisory-lock gates in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. Both are keyed on the (item, warehouse) pair. Row-level `for update` locks and a deadlock retry sit underneath them. I read the code but didn't run it. **1. `repost_gate(item_code, warehouse)` (line 276), for background reposts** - `repost_future_sle()` wraps each `update_entries_after(...)` call in `with repost_gate(item_code, warehouse):` (line 335). - On Postgres, when `frappe.db` has `advisory_lock`, the gate calls `frappe.db.advisory_lock(("stock_repost", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. This is a session-level advisory lock. - The lock is taken before the inner `... for update` row locks. Two reposts of the same pair therefore take turns instead of locking rows in conflicting order and deadlocking. - The key is a tuple, so a colon in an item code or warehouse name can't make two different pairs share a lock. - The same wait also stops two jobs from recomputing the same item-warehouse valuation at once. The row locks are what guarantee correctness; the gate only reduces deadlocks and retries. - Where `advisory_lock` or Postgres isn't available, it returns `nullcontext()`, so there is no gate. MariaDB uses the plain deadlock-retry path. - `REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT` is 300 seconds. That is well under the 1800-second job timeout. A worker that waits too long fails with a recoverable `QueryTimeoutError` and re-queues. - The synchronous `repost_current_voucher` path used on submit is deliberately not gated. That avoids blocking a submit behind a background repost, so it relies on the existing deadlock retry. **2. `sle_processing_gate(item_code, warehouse)` (line 292), for all stock writes** - It is called from line 120 and from line 613 (`update_entries_after`). - It takes `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("stock-sle", item_code, warehouse), timeout=...)`. This lock is scoped to the transaction, can be taken again within it, and is released at commit or rollback. - MariaDB gets this serialization from the gap locks on its locking reads of the previous stock ledger entry (SLE). Postgres locking reads can't see rows another transaction is still inserting. - Without the gate, two concurrent writers would compute from the same stale previous SLE, and the loser's Bin write would be lost. - If `transaction_advisory_lock` doesn't exist, it falls back to the older serialization-failure retries. **3. Recovery in `repost()` (`erpnext/stock/doctype/repost_item_valuation/repost_item_valuation.py`, line 530)** - It commits the "In Progress" status first, then runs the repost. - On an exception it rolls back. If the exception is an instance of `RecoverableErrors` (deadlock, lock or query timeout, job timeout), the status goes back to "In Progress" so the job is retried. Anything else is marked "Failed". - Classification is by exception type. The old traceback string-match only recognised MariaDB's "Deadlock found" and missed Postgres's "deadlock detected", which made those jobs fail permanently. `test_repost_item_valuation.py` covers this with `QueryDeadlockError("deadlock detected")`. - `repost_future_sle` saves progress after each pair through `update_args_in_repost_item_valuation` (`current_index`, `reposting_data_file`) and commits, which releases the session lock. A retried job resumes from that point rather than recomputing everything.

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext serializes concurrent batch valuations for the same item and warehouse with a transaction-scoped advisory lock. I read `BatchNoValuation` in `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py` and the repost entry points in `repost_item_valuation.py`. I didn't read the lock helper `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock` itself, which lives in Frappe rather than this repo. **The lock.** `BatchNoValuation.calculate_avg_rate()` (around line 818) is where the batch average rate is computed. - For incoming entries (`actual_qty > 0`) it takes `total_amount` from the Serial and Batch Bundle. It reads no history, so it needs no lock. - For outgoing entries it must derive the average rate from the item-warehouse history. If `frappe.db.db_type == "postgres"`, it first calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("batch-valuation", sle.item_code, sle.warehouse))`. - The lock is held until the transaction commits or rolls back. A second job valuing the same item-warehouse therefore waits until the first has committed. It then reads the committed history, so it doesn't compute the rate from stale data. **Why advisory locks on PostgreSQL.** The code comments give the reasoning: - On MariaDB, `get_batch_stock_before_date()` adds `.for_update()` to the grouped query. Together with gap locks, that serializes the work. - PostgreSQL has no gap locks. `FOR UPDATE` is also invalid together with `GROUP BY`. Row-locking the whole history would write a lock marker on every tuple. - So on PostgreSQL the query skips `for_update()` (`if frappe.db.db_type != "postgres"`). The advisory lock does the serializing instead. - Because the lock key is always (item, warehouse), every job takes the same lock for the same pair. This avoids deadlocks from locking rows in different orders. **The reposting layer.** - `RecoverableErrors = (JobTimeoutException, QueryDeadlockError, QueryTimeoutError)` in `repost_item_valuation.py` marks deadlocks and timeouts as recoverable. A job that still hits one is retried rather than failed. - `execute_repost_item_valuation()` enqueues a single Scheduled Job Type. It runs either `repost_entries` or `run_parallel_reposting`, depending on `enable_parallel_reposting` in Stock Reposting Settings. - `get_repost_item_valuation_entries()` picks Queued and In Progress docs ordered by posting datetime, then creation. That keeps the processing order deterministic. - `deduplicate_similar_repost()` (seen only as a call at line 908) is meant to skip duplicate reposts. I didn't open `run_parallel_reposting`, `repost_entries` or `deduplicate_similar_repost`, so I haven't confirmed how they partition or dedupe work.

final answer
Workers never claim rows themselves. A single dispatcher claims each row first, and the workers only run rows that are already marked as claimed. The code is in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. The Period Closing Voucher creates a "Process Period Closing Voucher" (PPCV) document. Each date/report-type row is a child row, "Process Period Closing Voucher Detail" (PPCVD), and each row has its own `status` field. 1. **Row locking at claim time.** `initialize_parallel_threads(docname)` selects up to 4 PPCVD rows with `status = "Queued"`, ordered by `parentfield, idx, processing_date`. It uses `.for_update(skip_locked=True)`, which is `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. A concurrent dispatcher therefore skips rows that another transaction has locked, instead of waiting on them or taking them. 2. **Status change before enqueue.** For each selected row, the code sets `status = "Running"` and then calls `frappe.enqueue(process_individual_date, queue="long", enqueue_after_commit=True, row_name=x.name, …)`. It then commits (`frappe.db.commit()`). The commit keeps the transaction short, which the source comment says avoids REPEATABLE READ concurrency errors. Because the row is already `Running` and committed, no later query for `Queued` rows can return it. 3. **One row per call.** `schedule_next_date(docname)` uses the same pattern with `limit(1)`, `for_update(skip_locked=True)`, the `Running` update and a commit. Each worker calls it when it finishes, so the next date is picked up one at a time. 4. **Worker-side check.** `process_individual_date(docname, row_name, date, report_type, parentfield)` begins by re-reading the row's status. It returns immediately unless the status is `"Running"`. This stops a stale or duplicate job, for example one for a row that was paused or cancelled. 5. **Summary job.** When no `Queued` rows remain and all rows are `Completed`, `schedule_next_date` enqueues `summarize_and_post_ledger_entries`. It uses `job_name = f"summarize_{docname}"` and `is_job_running`, so the summary is only queued once. The pause, cancel and resume functions (`pause_pcv_processing`, `cancel_pcv_processing`, `resume_pcv_processing`) move rows between `Queued` and `Running` so that stuck rows can be released. I didn't check whether `is_job_running` itself is race-free.

final answer
Two workers never pick up the same date/report-type row, for three reasons. Rows are claimed under a locking query, marked "Running" in a short transaction, and each worker re-checks that status before it starts. I read the code but did not run it. All of this is in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. 1. **Rows to claim.** On submit, `ProcessPeriodClosingVoucher.on_submit` calls `start_pcv_processing`. `populate_processing_tables` has already created one child row per date and report type ("Profit and Loss" and "Balance Sheet"). It also creates `z_opening_balances` rows for the first closing voucher. Each row is a `Process Period Closing Voucher Detail` with status "Queued". 2. **Claiming a row with a lock.** `schedule_next_date(docname)` selects the next "Queued" row for that parent. It orders by `parentfield`, `idx` and `processing_date`, takes `limit(1)`, and uses `.for_update(skip_locked=True)`. That is `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. A worker that finds a row already locked by another transaction skips it instead of waiting, so it can't claim that row. 3. **Marking it Running.** Right after selecting, the claiming worker sets the row's `status` to "Running" with `frappe.db.set_value`. It then commits (outside tests) so the transaction stays short. This avoids REPEATABLE READ concurrency errors, and later selectors no longer see the row as "Queued". 4. **Handing off to a worker.** `frappe.enqueue` sends the row to `process_individual_date` on the "long" queue. It passes `row_name`, `date`, `report_type` and `parentfield`, with `enqueue_after_commit=True`. The job therefore only exists once the "Running" status is committed. 5. **Guard in the worker.** `process_individual_date` begins by reading the row's status and returns immediately unless it is "Running". A duplicate or stale job therefore does nothing. 6. **Completion and chaining.** The worker computes the GL summary for that date and report type. It stores the result in `closing_balance`, sets the row to "Completed" and commits. It then calls `schedule_next_date` again to claim the next "Queued" row. 7. **Final step.** When no "Queued" rows remain and every row is "Completed", `schedule_next_date` enqueues `summarize_and_post_ledger_entries`. It uses the job name `summarize_<docname>` and checks `is_job_running`, so that final step is also not started twice.

final answer
Two workers never pick up the same date/report-type row because of three things. The picker claims a row with a locking query, it changes the row's status in the same step, and each job checks that status before it starts. The code is in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. The Period Closing Voucher's GL processing runs through the `Process Period Closing Voucher` doctype. Each date and report-type pair is a row in its child table, `Process Period Closing Voucher Detail`, with a status of `Queued`, `Running` or `Completed`. 1. **Locking pick query.** `initialize_parallel_threads(docname)` takes up to 4 rows. `schedule_next_date(docname)` takes 1 row each time it is called. Both select rows with `status = "Queued"` for that parent, ordered by `parentfield`, `idx` and `processing_date`. Both end the query with `.for_update(skip_locked=True)`. This is `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. A row that another transaction has already locked is skipped, so two callers selecting at the same moment get different rows. 2. **Claiming the row.** Before enqueueing, the picker sets the row's status to `"Running"` with `frappe.db.set_value`. It then calls `frappe.db.commit()`, except in tests. The comment in the code says this keeps the transaction short and avoids REPEATABLE READ concurrency errors. After the commit, later pickers no longer see the row as `Queued`. 3. **Enqueue after commit.** The picker calls `frappe.enqueue(... process_individual_date ...)` on the `long` queue with `enqueue_after_commit=True`. The job is therefore only queued once the `Running` status has been committed. Each job gets its own `row_name`, `date`, `report_type` and `parentfield`. 4. **Check inside the worker.** `process_individual_date` first reads the row's status and returns immediately unless it is `"Running"`. A duplicate or stale job therefore does nothing if the row has been paused, cancelled or already completed. When no `Queued` rows are left, `schedule_next_date` checks whether every row is `Completed`. If so, it enqueues `summarize_and_post_ledger_entries` once. It guards that job with the name `summarize_<docname>` and the `is_job_running` check, so the final summary is not duplicated either.
